
[PDF page 1]
TWO GLOBAL STABILITY THEOREMS FOR SMALL-DATA3D
COMPRESSIBLE EULER SOLUTIONS: UNIQUE MAXIMAL GLOBALLY
HYPERBOLIC DEVELOPMENTS WITH GRADIENT-BLOWUP&SMOOTH
GLOBAL EXISTENCE IN THE ENTIRE SPACETIME
LEONARDO ABBRESCIA, JARED SPECK, AND DONGXIAO YU
Abstract.We prove a pair of complementary small-data stability theorems that yield the global
structure – to the future and past – of classical solutions to the isentropic, spherically symmetric
3Dcompressible Euler equations. We allow for any equation of state with positive sound speed,
except our shock-formation results do not hold for the Chaplygin gas. We expect that our results
can be extended away from symmetry with the help of a robust set of techniques developed by
Christodoulou, Luk–Speck, and Abbrescia–Speck.
In our first theorem, we study the Cauchy problem for open sets of smooth data that are
perturbations of trivial data with vanishing velocity and constant positive density. The perturbed
data have finite kinetic energy (the initial velocity is even allowed to be zero), and the perturbed
density is equal to a positive constant plus an “asymptotically flat tail” of size˚α/rasr→ ∞, where
˚αis a small, signed, non-vanishing constant. Our main theorem yields a complete description of
the existence and uniqueness of the maximal globally hyperbolic development (MGHD) of the data,
to the future and the past, for solutions that develop gradient-singularities on hypersurfaces that
extend to spatial infinity. This is the first MGHD existence-uniqueness-stability result for shock-
forming solutions for any multi-dimensional quasilinear hyperbolic wave-like system. While prior
works have yielded portions of “candidate” MGHDs, it is known that neither the existence nor
the uniqueness of an MGHD can be inferred from local considerations; in an appendix, we show
that for many quasilinear hyperbolic PDEs, including the compressible Euler equations, one must
construct the entire MGHD and understand the structure of its boundary to know that it exists
and is unique.
In our second theorem, we consider similar initial data, except the small 1/rdensity tail has
the opposite sign. We prove global existence in the entire spacetime, to the future and past. The
solution’s asymptotic behavior towards null infinity is not linear, but rather is distorted by a loga-
rithmic bending of the sound cones away from the flat Minkowski ones, leading to logarithmically
enhanced dispersion. This is the first small-data global existence result in the entire spacetime for
any 3Dquasilinear hyperbolic wave-like system that fails to satisfy the null condition and the weak
null condition. The main mechanism of stabilization is a global-in-spacetime, though decaying,
rarefaction effect, tied to the 1/rtail. In both theorems, we use nonlinear geometric optics to
show that the nonlinear radiation field has a global sign and to show that relative to a system of
geometric coordinates tied to an eikonal function, the nonlinear terms exhibit good null structure,
leading to decay.

1.Introduction
We prove a pair ofsmall-data global stability theorems for classical solutions to the Cauchy
problem for the 3Disentropic compressible Euler equations in spherical symmetry:

whereϱ:R×[0,∞)→[0,∞) is the density,v r :R×[0,∞)→Ris the radial velocity component,
c=c(ϱ)>0 is the speed of sound, and (t, r)∈R×[0,∞) are standard spherical coordinate
functions. The speed of sound is determined by the equation of state (see Sect. 1.4). By “small-
data,” we mean that we study perturbations – with a small-amplitudenon-vanishing tail– of
“trivial” solutions featuring constant positive density and vanishing velocity; see below.
For open sets of smooth data (at timet= 0), our work affirmatively answers the following
questions about solutions, of fundamental mathematical and physical interest:
•Is thereanylargest possible subsetM Max of spacetime on which a classical
(i.e.,C 1 or smoother) solution exists and is uniquely determined by the initial
data?
•If so, is the regionM Max itself uniquely determined by the initial data?
•IsM Max the entire spacetime? If not, what happens along∂M Max, i.e., why
can’t the solution be uniquely extended as a classical solution past it?
In our first theorem, we show the existence and uniqueness of a classical 1 maximal globally
hyperbolic development(MGHD) for open sets of smooth data that lead to shock-forming solutions.
In particular,M Max exists and is uniquely determined by the data, but it is not the entire spacetime.
The notion of an MGHD relies on the fact that the causal structure of the compressible Euler
equations is governed by asolution-dependent Lorentzian metric, a fact that is not true for a
general hyperbolic PDE; see Appendix B. Roughly, an MGHD is a largest possible classical solution
+ region that is determined by the initial data, and fascinating phenomena can occur along the
boundary. Specifically, for the solutions we study, the MGHD future-boundary consists of a singular
portion extending to spatial infinity, along which gradient-blowup (i.e., shock formation) occurs,
and a pre-compact Cauchy horizon, i.e., a null hypersurface that emanates from the point 2 of first
blowup and terminates along the axis of symmetry{r= 0}. The two future-boundary portions are
continuously – but not differentiably – joined at a corner (the aforementioned point of first blowup)
1Although our work here concerns only classical solutions, it turns out that the global structure of the classical
MGHD is important forconstructinga class of weak, piecewise-smooth solutions that emanate from the gradient-
blowup region; see [6] for further discussion.
2In the “lifted” picture inR 1+3, this point corresponds to a two-dimensional sphere.
2

[PDF page 3]
that we call thecrease. The solution behaves similarly for negative times (see Remark 1.5) and
thus analogous results hold for the MGHD past-boundary; see Fig. 1.
In our second theorem, for related open sets of small, smooth data, we prove classical global
existence to thefuture and past, i.e.,M Max is the entire spacetime. Both theorems hold for any
equation of state such that the sound speed is positive when the density is positive, except for our
shock-formation MGHD theorem, which does not apply to the Chaplygin gas equation of state. 3
The novelty of this paper can be summarized as follows:
Both of our theorems yield the first truly global, small-data results of their type
for any quasilinear wave-like PDE system in 3Dthat fails to satisfy Klainerman’s
null condition [27] as well as Lindblad–Rodnianski’s weak null condition [29]. The
global sign of the radiation fieldis the key mechanism driving the behavior of the
main terms in the equations, while (sound) wave dispersion and nonlinear geometric
optics allow us to control the nonlinear error terms.
While the existence of at least one MGHD follows from a Zorn’s lemma argument (see The-
orem B.13), it should be appreciated that the uniqueness of an MGHD is a highly nontrivial,
global, geometric phenomenon that does not always hold. In fact, in principle, it is possible
that there is not even a single unique globally hyperbolic development of the data, let alone a
maximal one; see Sect. B.4.5. While the celebrated works of Choquet-Bruhat [14] and Choquet-
Bruhat–Geroch [13] show that a unique MGHD always exists for Einstein’s equations, in the re-
markable paper [19], Eperon–Reall–Sbierski gave an example of a quasilinear wave equation in
1DandC ∞ data that evolves into two distinct globally hyperbolic developments such that the
corresponding classical solutionsdisagreeon part of the intersection of the two domains, a hor-
rifying breakdown in classical determinism. For the shock-forming solutions in our first main
theorem, we were able to prove uniqueness only because we derived the global structure of the
entire MGHD past- and future- boundaries and proved that in (t, r) coordinates, each of the two
boundaries is the graph of a continuous function, unlike the example in [19]; see Appendix B for
further discussion.
In both theorems, the initial data aresmall, smooth perturbations of data with vanishing velocity
and a constant positive density. More precisely, the perturbed velocity data decays to 0 at least as
fast as 1/r2, leading to finite kinetic energy,4 and the density data converges to a positive constant
at the “asymptotically flat” rate of 1/r. Importantly, in terms of an expansion of the density
data in terms of powers of 1/r(forrlarge), the 1/rterm isnon-zero, with an arbitrary small but
non-vanishing amplitude. This allows us to exploit various monotonicity properties in theradiation
fieldgenerated by the main part of the density perturbation tail, the 1/rpart. In fact, in both
theorems, the radiation field has a global sign , though it decays at a polynomial rate away from
the wave zone; see Remark 7.2.
It is of interest to note that in both theorems, the data arenotclose to that of a “simple wave,”
i.e., near time 0, the solution features both inwards-moving and outwards-moving sound waves, in
nearly equal proportions. More precisely, in terms of a system of spherical Riemann invariantsR ±
(see Def. 2.1), we choose the data for each ofR (+),R (−) to be approximately equal; see e.g., (63a)–
(63b). This corresponds to the “very small” velocity data highlighted above. While the dynamics
ofR (+) eventually dominates to the future (andR (−) dominates to the past), the domination does
not become evident untildispersive effectstake over, eventually leading to asymptotically distinct
3A Chaplygin gas has an equation of statep=C 1 − C2

Chaplygin gas), shock formation is not expected in this case, though our global existence theorem still applies (albeit
with simpler asymptotics compared to other equations of state.

behavior forR (+) andR (−). Indeed, the crux of the PDE analysis is aboutcompetition between
dispersive effects and resonant nonlinearities.
1.1.Brief motivation and context.Here we provide brief motivation and context for our re-
sults. In Sect. 1.2, we provide additional context and further explain some key connections between
our work and the vast literature on the structure of classical solutions to 3Dcompressible Euler
equations and related nonlinear wave equations.
Our MGHD results for shock-forming solutions are especially motivated by Christodoulou’s
groundbreaking monograph [16], in which he derived a large portion of a future-MGHD for open
sets of shock-forming solutions – without symmetry assumptions – to the 3Dirrotational and isen-
tropic relativistic Euler equations in the small-data regime, i.e., small perturbations of data with
constant positive density and vanishing velocity. The data-perturbations in [16] did not have a 1/r
tail, but rather were compactly supported and hence, thanks to domain of dependence considera-
tions, the solutions were trivial in the exterior of a flat, outgoing sound cone. In [16], Christodoulou
developed many important techniques for studying MGHDs, tied to nonlinear geometric optics, and
offered a vision for the future of the field. The portion of an MGHD derived in [16, Theorem 15.1,
Proposition 15.3] was not explicitly constructed, but rather was described as a union of globally
hyperbolic developments of the data tied to an uncountably infinite family of foliations of a portion
of spacetime by sound cones and flat, spacelike hypersurfaces that terminate on the “gradient-
blowup” portion of the MGHD boundary. Thanks to the properties of the solution induced by our
1/rdata-tails, our description of the MGHD is fully constructive and complete, which in particular
allows us to conclude uniqueness. Another crucial result of Christodoulou’s monograph is [16, The-
orem 13.1], which shows that in the small-data regime, for compactly supported, isentropic, and
irrotational data perturbations, away from the interior region, the only kinds of singularities that
can form in principle are shocks due to gradient-blowup caused by the infinite density of outgoing
characteristics. Put differently, Christodoulou provedconditional global existencein an exterior
region, i.e., thanks to dispersive effects, global existence holds as long as outgoing characteristics
never become infinitely dense. To date, his analytical framework is the only one that has been able
to prove conditional global existence; all subsequent works on multi-dimensional shock formation
either used his framework or were restricted to a limited data regime that enjoyed additional prop-
erties guaranteeing that a shock quickly forms in the flow. Although we do not explicitly state
an analogous result in this paper, the proofs of our main theorems show that a conditional global
existence result, similar to the one in [16, Theorem 13.1], holds for data perturbations with a small
1/rtail – in the entire spacetime – albeit with more complicated asymptotics induced by the tail.
Our global existence results are motivated by the backwards scattering results derived in third
author’s paper [43], whichconstructedan interesting class of candidate “asymptotic profiles” and
used them to construct non-trivial future-global solutions to the 3Dirrotational and isentropic
compressible Euler equations as well as related nonlinear wave equations. We clarify that [43]
yielded an existence result – only to the future – and the stability of the constructed solutions,
from the point of view of the Cauchy problem, was not investigated in that paper.
Although we have treated only the compressible Euler equations in detail, our methods are robust
and applicable to other quasilinear wave equations that fail to satisfy the null condition; the novelty
of this paper is our discovery that a regime of “ 1
r tail” initial data can be thecauseof tractable
global, nonlinear dynamics tied to the radiation field having a sign, rather than the precise details
of the equations. For this reason, we expect that our results can be generalized to other classes
of hyperbolic PDEs besides wave equations. Moreover, even though our results are in spherical
symmetry, they are compatible with a set of robust multi-dimensional techniques developed by
Christodoulou [16], Speck–Luk [32, 41], and Abbrescia–Speck [1, 4, 5], which we anticipate can be
4

[PDF page 5]
used to generalize our results away from symmetry. We especially highlight that the techniques de-
veloped by Abbrescia–Speck apply in particular to perturbations of symmetric solutions (including
the ones studied in this paper), a class of solutions for which various convexity assumptions used
in other approaches to studying shock formation do not hold. To keep the paper a manageable
length, and to help readers navigate the main ideas, we have treated in detail a well-chosen open
set of initial data, based on perturbing a specific one-parameter family of profiles. In Appendix A,
we outline how to generalize our results to a much larger class of data.
1.2.Heuristic description of the main theorems and context.We now describe our results
in more detail, though still at a heuristic level. Readers interested in the detailed theorems can
immediately jump to Theorems 10.1 and 11.3.
1.2.1.Globally stable, unique maximal globally hyperbolic developments for shock-forming solutions.
In our first theorem, using a constructive approach, we provide the first proof of existenceand
uniqueness of themaximal globally hyperbolic development(MGHD) of data for shock-forming
solutions to the equations. We refer to Appendix B for a rigorous definition of an MGHD and
corresponding background material. Here, we will only provide a heuristic description. Roughly,
for a hyperbolic wave-like system, aglobally hyperbolic development(GHD) of given smooth data is
a region of spacetimeM⊂R 1+3 that is equal to the union of the domain of dependence of all of its
points, and on which the solution exists classically. A GHD is calledmaximalif it is inextendible
as a GHD. That is,Mis maximal if for any GHD ˇM⊂R 1+3 suchM⊂ ˇM, it must hold that
ˇM=M. One can therefore view an MGHD as a largest spacetime region on which fixed initial data
launches a classical solution. In other words, understanding an MGHD is equivalent to one of the
most fundamental questions in PDE theory: to what extent can one construct a classical solution
from smooth data? The (possibly empty)boundaryof an MGHD consists of the spacetime points
on which the corresponding classical solution develops a singularity or points beyond which the
initial data no longer uniquely determines a classical solution. We already highlight the following
point, fleshed out below:
In principle, there might be twodistinctmaximal classical solutions, defined on re-
gionsM 1 andM 2, such that the corresponding maximal classical solutions disagree
at some points inM 1 ∩M 2. Such a breakdown in classical determinism actually
happens in some model quasilinear wave equation solutions launched by smooth
data; see Sect. B.4.4. In our main theorem, we show that this does not happen, at
least for the open sets of data that we study.
We now state a rough version our main MGHD theorem; see Theorem 10.1 for the precise
statement and Fig. 1 for a graphical depiction of the results.
Theorem 1.1(Rough version of the main MGHD theorem).For the spherically symmetric isen-
tropic compressible Euler equations in3D, under anyC 4 equation of state with positive sound speed
except the Chaplygin gas, there exists an open set ofC 3, “compressive” data with small velocity,
finite kinetic energy, and with density that is a small perturbation of a positive constant ϱand that
converges to ϱat the asymptotically flat rate of ˚α
r asr→ ∞, where ˚α>0is any sufficiently small
amplitude. Given such data, the following results hold (see Fig. 1):
(1)(Classical solution). With respect to the differential structure of the standard Cartesian co-
ordinates onR 1+3, there is a spherically symmetric maximal globally hyperbolic development
5

[PDF page 6]
MMax ⊊ R1+3, on which the solution is classical. 5 By globally hyperbolic, we mean with re-
spect to the acoustical metricg, the solution-dependent Lorentzian metric that determines
the intrinsic causal structure of the solution; see(448)–(450).
(2)(MGHD boundary). Viewed as a subset of(t, r)-coordinate space, the boundary ofM Max
consists of the following sets, depicted in Fig. 1:
•A future-crease∂ −BFuture, which is the unique spacetime point (corresponding to a
2Dsphere inR 1+3) whose temporal component is the first positive time for which
the gradient of the physical fluid variables (i.e., the velocity and density) blows up,
while the undifferentiated fluid variables remain bounded. 6 There is also a past crease
∂+BPast, which is the unique spacetime point whose temporal component is the first
negative time for which the gradient of the fluid variables blows up, while the fluid
variables remain bounded. The density of the future-outgoing characteristics is infinite
at∂ −BFuture, and the density of the past-outgoing characteristics is infinite at∂ +BPast.
The two creases are not part ofM Max, but they are part of its topological closure:
∂−BFuture, ∂+BPast ⊂ M

a hypersurface-portion inR 1+3) that emanates from the future-crease and flows to-
wards future temporal and spatial infinity, along which the gradient of the physical
fluid variables continue to blow-up, while the undifferentiated fluid variables remain
bounded.7 There is also a past-singular boundaryB Past, which is aC 1 curve-portion
(i.e., a hypersurface-portion inR 1+3) that emanates from the past-crease and flows
towards past temporal and spatial infinity, along which the gradient of the physical
fluid variables continues to blow-up, while the undifferentiated fluid variables remain
bounded. The density of the future-outgoing characteristics is infinite alongB Future,
and the density of the past-outgoing characteristics is infinite alongB Past. The two
singular boundaries are not part ofM Max, but they are part of its topological closure:
BFuture,B Past ⊂ M

(3)(Maximality and Uniqueness).M Max is maximal (i.e., there is no way to uniquely extend
the classical solution beyond it), and the regionM Max itself as well as the solution on it are
uniquely determined by the data. Moreover,anyother globally hyperbolic development of
the data is defined only on asubsetofM Max, and the corresponding solution agrees with
the one from Point1.

(4)(Behavior of the outgoing characteristics). There is an eikonal functionudefined in a large
subset ofM Max away from the origin, extending to spatial infinity, such that for positive
times, the level sets{u=constant}are outgoing characteristics that are distortions of
the flat characteristics{t−r=constant}by error terms that grow logarithmically in time.
Relative to the(t, u)coordinates, the nonlinear solution decays like a linear wave towards the
trivial solution with vanishing velocity and constant density ϱ. Similarly, for negative times,
the level sets of the eikonal function are logarithmic distortions of the flat characteristics
{t+r=constant}.
(5)(Global compression). The density of the future- and past- outgoing characteristics grows as
|t|increase, until it becomes infinite on the two singular boundaries. That is, the solution is
globally compressive. The compression is caused by the radiation field having a global sign ;
see Remark 7.2.
Remark 1.2(Simplified notation in the bulk of the paper).The bulk of the paper concerns PDE
analysis for timest≥0. To simplify the notation, in the bulk, we will sometimes drop the label
“Future” from various symbols, i.e., we denote the timeT Future
Crease from Fig. 1 byT Crease, we denote
the radial valueR Future
Crease from Fig. 1 byR Crease, etc.
At its most primitive level, Theorem 1.1 is a stable shock-formation result for solutions arising
from smooth data. In recent years, there has been an explosion of mathematical activity tied to
shock-formation problems,8 especially in multi-dimensions. The first multi-dimensional results were
Alinhac’s important papers [7,8] on quasilinear wave equations for “non-degenerate” shock-forming
solutions that enjoy convexity properties. His approach, which relied on a Nash–Moser iteration
scheme to close the energy estimates, was able to follow the solution until the flat constant-time
hypersurface ΣTShock of first-blowup, but not to larger regions of spacetime. Thanks to the convexity
assumptions, within Σ TShock, the singular points wereisolated, i.e., Alinhac derived a finite number
of isolated points on the boundary of an MGHD.
As we highlighted in Sect. 1.1, in his breakthrough monograph [16], Christodoulou developed
techniques much stronger than Alinhac’s, with a profound geometric perspective, and applied them
to study large portions of an MGHD, for small-data shock-forming solutions to the wave equations
of irrotational and isentropic relativistic fluid mechanics. Crucially, Christodoulou avoided Nash–
Moser-type estimates, and instead closed the energy estimates with the help of techniques that he
co-developed with Klainerman in their celebrated proof of the stability of the Minkowski spacetime
[17]; this is a main reason why he was able to study a much larger MGHD portion compared to
Alinhac. The portions of an MGHD studied in [16] featured a large, though implicit, portion of an
MGHD boundary where gradient-blowup occurs. No Cauchy horizon was derived in [16], but the
“tilted” foliations used there yielded a region of existence close to one. New formulations of the fluid
equations exhibiting remarkable structures were derived in [32, 41], and those equations were used
to prove the first stable shock formation result for 2Disentropic compressible fluids with vorticity
in [31]. For 2Disentropic compressible fluids with vorticity under adiabatic equations of state,
with data of large gradient size 1
ϵ that enjoy an Alinhac-type convexity assumption, [38] provided a
description of a portion of an MGHD that lies between two parallel constant-time slices separated
by distanceO(ϵ). This included anO(ϵ)-sized portion of a singular boundary, where gradient-
blowup occurs, and anO(ϵ)-size portion of a Cauchy horizon, where no singularity develops. [33]
proved stable shock formation for nearly plane symmetric solutions to the 3Dcompressible Euler
equations with vorticity and entropy, which is much more difficult than the 2Disentropic case
due to the presence of a vorticity stretching term and an entropy-gradient term in the equations,
8Other kinds of compressible Euler singularities, such as the celebrated “implosions” [34, 35], can also arise from
smooth data. Unlike shocks, implosions seem to be unstable.

both of which lead to a challenging regularity theory. The main results of [33] did not require any
convexity assumption, but the solutions were studied only until the time of first gradient-blowup,
i.e., most of the MGHD boundary was not studied there. Under the same setup in 3D, [1, 5]
derived anO(1)-size portion of both the singular boundary and the Cauchy horizon, as well as the
full structure of the crease, using only a “transversal convexity” assumption, which even in 1Dis
essential to ensure that a continuum of characteristics does not collapse into a low-dimensional set;
were that to occur, it would be very challenging to study the questions of existence and uniqueness
of an MGHD. Crucially, the techniques developed in [1, 5] are applicable to perturbations – away
from symmetry – of the solutions that we study in this paper. The top-order regularity theory in
[1, 5] relied on remarkable energy identities derived in [4], which allow one to propagate an extra
8

[PDF page 9]
degree worth of regularity for the vorticity and entropy on arbitrary globally hyperbolic domains,
which in turn allows one to treat the flow as a perturbation of an irrotational and isentropic one.
We also mention that for solutions that enjoy an Alinhac-type convexity assumption, there is
another approach, initiated in [11], to following compressible Euler solutions to the time of first
gradient-blowup, based on self-similarity and modulation parameters. For deriving the full structure
of an MGHD, especially in the context of solutions that exhibit acompetition between dispersion
and nonlinearities, the framework initiated by Christodoulou in [16] is by far the most powerful one
to date. In particular, it interacts well with the large array of techniques that have been developed
to study the long-time behavior of solutions to wave-like equations, and it features many geometric
ingredients that are of immense value for following solutions up to the MGHD boundary. There are
many other works that address various aspects of shock formation; for a more detailed discussion
of the history of the field as well as additional context, we refer readers to the surveys [3,6,23].
A surprisingly subtle fact concerning the uniqueness aspect of the MGHD from Theorem 1.1 is
that uniquenesscannotbe deduced from analysis on localized regions in spacetime or even from
knowingexistence! In fact, the proof of uniqueness ofM Max fundamentally relies on knowing
the complete global structure and topology of its boundary∂M Max, as is depicted in Fig. 1. We
discuss these issues in detail in Appendix B. Here, we highlight that in the breakthrough paper
[19], Eperon–Reall–Sbierski proved that MGHD-uniqueness holds provided thatM Max everywhere
lies on one side of its boundary; see Theorem B.17 for the precise statement. Surprisingly, for a
given quasilinear wave equation, this property isnot guaranteed to holdfor all MGHDs of smooth
data. More precisely, in that same paper, the authors gave a remarkable constructive example
in one spatial dimension of a quasilinear wave equation for whicha fixedC ∞ initial data evolved
classicallyin two distinct globally hyperbolic fashionson regions of spacetime which overlap.
More precisely, they found a smooth initial data set which gave rise to two classical solutions
Φ1,Φ 2, each respectively defined on regionsM 1,M 2 ⊂R 1+1 such thatM 1 ̸=M 2 and such that
there exists ap∈M 1 ∩M 2 with Φ 1(p)̸= Φ 2(p). Their result is an example of a strong form of
failure of classical determinism, and a key conclusion of Theorem 1.1 is that such failure doesnot
occur for the set of compressible Euler data that we treat in this paper.
The above discussion suggests the following open problems, of intense mathematical and physical
interest:
•(Non-unique MGHDs). Is there any smooth data for the compressible Euler
equations that yields two distinct, intersecting globally hyperbolic develop-
mentsM 1 andM 2 such thatM 1 ̸=M 2, and such that the corresponding
solutionsdisagreeat some pointp∈M 1 ∩M 2?
•(Not even a single unique GHD). Is there any smooth data for the compressible
Euler equations on a spacelike Cauchy hypersurface Σ such thatthere is no
neighborhoodOofΣ on which one can guarantee that all classical solutions
that are defined on globally hyperbolic subsets must agree at every point inO?
While at first glance, the second question above might seem like it follows from local well-posedness,
it in fact does not! See Sect. B.4.5 for further discussion. An example would show that for the given
data, there is no neighborhood of the Cauchy hypersurface on which classical determinism holds.
1.2.2.Global existence.In our second main theorem, we consider a related but distinct set of initial
data (see Remark 1.4) and prove global existence to the future and past. 9 Here we give a rough
statement of the theorem; see Theorem 11.3 for the precise result.
9This is also an MGHD existence and uniqueness theorem. In this case, the MGHD is the solution on the entire
spacetime, so there is no boundary.
9

[PDF page 10]
Theorem 1.3(Rough version of the main global existence theorem).For the spherically symmetric
isentropic compressible Euler equations in3D, under anyC 3 equation of state with positive sound
speed, there exists an open set of 10 C2, “rarefactive” data with small velocity, finite kinetic energy,
and with density that is a small perturbation of a positive constant ϱand that converges to ϱat the
asymptotically flat rate of 1
r asr→ ∞. For the data, the following results hold.
(1)(Global existence). The corresponding classical 11 solution is global in space and time to the
future and the past.
(2)(Behavior of the outgoing characteristics). There is an eikonal functionudefined on{r≥
1}, such that fort≥0, the level sets{u=constant}are outgoing characteristics that
are distortions of the flat characteristics{t−r=constant}by error terms that grow 12
logarithmically in time. Fort≤0, the level sets{u=constant}are outgoing characteristics
that are distortions of the flat characteristics{t+r=constant}by error terms that grow
logarithmically in time. Relative to the(t, u)coordinates, the nonlinear solution decays, to
the future and past, like a linear wave, towards the trivial solution with vanishing velocity
and constant density ϱ.
(3)(Global rarefaction). The outgoing characteristics separate logarithmically in time as|t| →
∞, that is, the solution is globally rarefactive. The rarefaction is caused by the radiation
field having a global sign ; see Remark 7.2.
Remark 1.4(Connection between the data for the two theorems).Roughly speaking, relative to
a system of spherical Riemann invariants (see Def. 2.1), the initial data for Theorem 1.3 has the
opposite sign as the data for Theorem 1.1.
A surprising aspect of our global existence theorem is that equations (1a)–(1b) fail to satisfy the
null condition and the weak null condition. By this, we roughly mean that the once-differentiated
fluid equations always feature Riccati-type resonant interactions,13 aside from the case of the Chap-
lygin gas equation of state. Below, we discuss these resonant terms in more detail. In many clas-
sically studiedsmall-data problems for nonlinear hyperbolic PDEs, failure of the null condition
and weak null condition has been shown to lead to singularity formation tied to the presence of
the resonant terms. However, resonant interactions have previously been exploited to showfuture-
global existence when the data are sufficientlylarge and have a “good” sign. In particular, there
are important compressible Euler papers showing that for adiabatic equations of state with initially
small density and an initiallylarge, signedvelocity,future-global existence holds. For example, in
[20], Grassin assumed that the initial density is small and that the initial velocity gradient∇v,
viewed as a matrix, has spectrum that is bounded from below, strictly away from 0. He then proved
future-global existence and dispersive estimates for the solution, due to spreading effects tied to
the large velocity gradient. However, his result does not extend to the past direction because the
spectrum of the initial∇vhas the wrong sign for past-global existence. In contrast, our initial ve-
locity can be arbitrarily small (even zero) with unsigned gradient tensor, and our global existence
result holds to the futureandpast. In a nutshell, for our global existence theorem, the initial data
10Note that Theorem 1.3 assumes one fewer derivatives compared to Theorem 1.1. The reason is that in the proof
of Theorem 1.1, the extra derivative is used only to derive the structure of the singular boundary and Cauchy horizon,
which are not present for the global solutions of Theorem 1.3.
11Although we only treatC 2 data in Theorem 1.3, standard propagation of regularity arguments could be used
to show that more regular data would lead to more regular solutions, i.e., the solutions are as smooth as the data,
except that ourC k-type norms involver-weights at the top order that degenerate nearr= 0.
12For the Chaplygin gas equation of state, these error terms grow much more slowly.
13For example, aside from the case of the Chaplygin gas equation of state, RHS (35a) always features a danger-
ous Riccati-term of typer(L R(+))2, stemming from the commutator term−r[L , L]R(+), studied in more detail in
equation (31).
10

[PDF page 11]
evolve to create a radiation field that drives the resonant terms to have agood rarefactive sign(see
Remark 7.2), to the past and future, which causes the outgoing characteristics to separate and
stabilizes the solution, though with distorted asymptotics. In our shock-forming MGHD theorem,
the radiation field has the opposite sign, leading to compression, and the resonant terms cause
gradient-blowup to occur in the solution, all the way out to spatial infinity, in a form of “controlled
blowup.”
Next, we highlight the important papers [21, 22], which proved future-global existence for open
sets ofexpandingsolutions to the compressible Euler equations under various adiabatic equations
of state. These are solutions in which the density is compactly supported and satisfies the physical
vacuum boundary condition at the fluid-vacuum boundary, and the velocity is approximately radial
and size 1 at the vacuum boundary. While not directly related to our work here, these results
provide fascinating examples of how outward velocity motion can future-globally stabilize fluids
(those solutions are possibly unstable to the past, due to the past-inward velocity motion).
A simplified model that featuresbothglobal compression and global rarefaction is our recent
work [2]. There, we studied the Cauchy problem for a quasilinear wave equation onR 1+1 that
fails to satisfy the null condition. The initial data on{t= 0}monotonically decreasesfrom a
positive constantc 1 >0 atx=−∞to a negative constantc 2 <0 atx= +∞. This monotonicity
forced the Riccati terms in the equation to have a globalcompressivesign towards the future and
a globalrarefactivesign towards the past. In particular, the solutions studied in [2] developed a
future-crease where a shock forms and from which a future-Cauchy horizon and future singular
boundary emanate. The Cauchy horizon extends all the way to negative spatial infinity (hence, it
fails to be pre-compact, which is different than in this paper) and the singular boundary extends
all the way to positive spatial infinity. On the other hand, for the same data, ast↓ −∞, the
characteristics smoothly and monotonically expand and the solution therefore enjoys smooth past-
global existence. As a whole, the boundary of this MGHD also enjoyed the global structure that
is sufficient to conclude MGHD-uniqueness. However, we stress that the results of [2] were much
easier to derive compared to those of this paper due to the lack of dispersive effects. We also stress
the difference in the topology on the ambient spacetime ofR 1+1 for [2] compared to the ambient
spherically symmetric spacetimeR×[0,∞) in the present paper, which in (t, r) coordinates has
a boundary{r= 0}. Morally, it is thereflectionof waves off of{r= 0}that preserves the sign
of the radiation field towards the future and past, leading to the nearly-time-symmetric results of
Theorems 1.1 and 1.3. There is no reflection of waves inR 1+1, and hence the solution is compressive
to the future and rarefactive to the past.
We also mention the interesting paper [12], in which the authors constructed some future-global,
spherically symmetric solutions to the compressible Euler equations for adiabatic equations of state
p=ϱ γ, 1<γ<3, in asupersonic regime, quite different than the small-data regime we treat here.
For the solutions in [12], both characteristic directions are outgoing to the future, e.g., the radial
variablerincreases along the future-directed integral curves of both characteristic vectorfields.
Consequently, fort≥0, no sound waves travel inwards towards the axis of symmetry{r= 0}, and
the authors were able to exploit various “domain-invariance” properties 14 to globally control the
solution. In contrast, our small-data solutions feature inwards and outwards moving sound waves,
for positive and negative times, and for the general equations of state that we treat, our only means
to control the solution is dispersive estimates and the sign of the radiation field.
Finally, we highlight Wang’s paper [42], in which she proved exterior future-global existence
for irrotational compressible fluids without symmetry, for a large class of “signed” initial data (a
“future-rarefaction” assumption was made on a term involving the density and its future-ingoing
null derivative). The data were non-trivial only in an annulus and hence domain of dependence
14This means that there are subsets of solution-space such that the solution remains in them for all future-times
if it starts in them.
11

[PDF page 12]
considerations imply that the solution is completely trivial in the exterior of the flat sound cone
emanating from the boundary of the annulus. Because of the sign assumption, the data have future-
rarefactive properties, but are not required to be small. The author proved that rarefactive-type
solutions exist for non-negative times in the non-trivial region between an future-outgoing inner
sound cone and the flat future-outgoing outer sound cone. The dynamics were not studied in the
interior region or past-direction, but it is likely that for at least some of the subsets of data, shock
singularities will develop to the past, due to the signed data causing “past-compression.” It is
also interesting to note that in the exterior vacuum region, the solutions in [42] areunstableunder
perturbations of the data due to small-data shock formation, as was shown by Christodoulou–Miao
in [18], using the techniques that Christodoulou pioneered in his monograph [16] on the relativistic
Euler equations.
As we have mentioned, the main novelty of Theorem 1.3 is that the compressible Euler equations
(1a)–(1b)fail to satisfy the null condition and the weak null condition(aside from the Chaplygin gas
equation of state) and hence there are resonant interactions. Resonant terms appear, for example,
in the evolution equation (35a) for theL derivative of the spherical Riemann invariantR (+), where
L =∂ t + (vr −c)∂ r is an ingoing null vectorfield andL=∂ t + (vr +c)∂ r is the complementary
outgoing null vectorfield; see Def. 2.2. The equation takes 15 the schematic form:
L(rLR(+)) =−r[L , L]R(+) +· · ·,(2)
and aside from the case of the Chaplygin gas equation of state, the commutator term−r[L , L]R(+)
on RHS (2) contains a dangerous, resonant Riccati-type term proportional tor(L R(+))2.
For 3Dwave equations thatdosatisfy the null condition, small-data global existence in 3Dis a
classic result proved independently by Klainerman and Christodoulou [15, 27]. Those results were
immensely influential and have since been generalized in an incredible number of directions, far too
many for us to exhaustively discuss here. A notable extension of them is Lindblad–Rodnianski’s
proof of the stability of Minkowski spacetime in wave coordinates [30], which remarkably simplified
the original proof [17]. In [30], the authors proved small-data global existence for a system of PDEs
without the null condition, but rather with a generalization of it coined the “weak null condition”
[29]. The weak null condition applies to systems of hyperbolic PDEs in which dangerous quadratic
nonlinearities are present, but they arenotRiccati-type self-interactions. That is, dangerous de-
rivative quadratic terms are allowed to appear, butneveron the right-hand side of an equation
whose principal left-hand part features the same unknown. In contrast, equation (2) fails to satisfy
even the weak null condition.
We highlight that like the data in our main results, the initial data in [30] featured a 1/rtail,
a Schwarzschild tail, which by the positive mass theorem had to be present. Before [30], almost
all global existence results for nonlinear wave equations concerned compactly supported data or
data that decayed faster than 1/r. The fact that the authors were able to prove global existence in
the presence of the weak null condition with a 1/rtail helped motivate us to investigate how the
tail affects equations for which even the weak null condition fails. It is interesting to note that in
[30], the 1/rtail was a “nuisance” in the PDE estimates that required careful care to handle. In
contrast, in this paper, the 1/rtail yields monotonic terms that allow us to understand the global
structure of the classical solution.
Generally throughout the literature, the presence of Riccati terms has been viewed as athreat
to small-data global existence. The reason is simply that the ODE ˙y=y 2, y(0) =y 0, features

None of the above discussion addressed whether singularities actually form in the nonlinear PDE
solutions when the null condition and weak null condition both fail. Fritz John’s work [24] showed
via a proof by contradiction that for a large class of 3Dnonlinear wave equations that fail to
satisfy the null condition and weak null condition, solutions launched by small, smooth, compactly
supported dataalwayslead to finite-time singularity formation. Sideris later proved a related
result for the 3Dcompressible Euler equations in his influential paper [39], in which he considered
smooth initial data that are compactly supported perturbations of trivial data with vanishing
velocity and entropy and constant positive density. He showed via a proof by contradiction that
when certain integrals of such data have a sign (roughly, a compressive sign), the solution must
blow up to the future in finite time. His work did not reveal the nature of the singularity or address
what happens for negative times (or equivalently, when the integrals have the opposite sign).
Moreover, in [25], John showed that for 3Dspherically symmetric quasilinear wave equations of
type∂ 2

authors prove that given arbitrary non-trivial, smooth compactly supported initial data for the 3D
irrotational and isentropic compressible Euler equations, if the data arere-scaledby a sufficiently
small constant, then the resulting solution forms a shock in finite time. Therefore, a key takeaway
of Theorem 1.3 is that without the compact support assumption on the data, the results of [25,28]
are not true in general.
Roughly, in Theorem 1.3, our integrals16 have the opposite sign compared to Sideris’ paper [39],
which causes the resonant Riccati-type terms to have a decay-inducing effect, rather than causing
blowup. There does not seem to be a simple way to capture this with an ODE model, since all

1.3.A brief overview of the analysis.In this section, we provide a brief overview of the analysis
we use to prove our main results. To capture wave dispersion (i.e., the decay of nonlinear fluid
sound waves), we find it convenient to use spherical Riemann invariantsR ±, defined below in
Def. 2.1. The densityϱand spherical velocity componentv r can be written as smooth functions
ofR ±, so to control the fluid, it suffices to controlR ±. The main advantage of the Riemann
invariants is that they satisfy a quasilinear, diagonal transport system, featuring factors of 1/rthat
generatewave dispersion; see (32a)–(32b). By our definitions,R (+) =R (−) ≡0 corresponds to a
solution with constant density ϱ >0 and vanishing velocity. The solutions in our main theorems are
perturbations of such a trivial solution. Our perturbations are such that the initial velocity isvery
small, with a tiny amplitude and decaying at least like 1/r 2 at spatial infinity (vanishing velocity
data is allowed); see Remark 5.2. Relatedly, in both of our theorems, the past- and future-dynamics
are almost mirror images of each other. We further this explain fact in the following remark.
Remark 1.5(It suffices to understand the solution fort≥0).The compressible Euler equations
(20a)–(20b) are invariant under the discrete symmetryt→ −tandv r → −v r, which leaves the
density initial data invariant and preserves our close-to-the-profile data-assumptions (63a)–(63b) in
the first theorem as well as our close-to-the-profile data-assumptions (305a)–(305b) in the second
theorem, and hence preserves the velocity-smallness assumptions clarified in Remark 5.2. In partic-
ular, the past-dynamics are nearly a mirror image of the future-dynamics. Moreover, this discrete
symmetry interchangesR (+) andR (−) as well as the null vectorfieldsLandL from Def. 2.2.
The following point will guide all the subsequent discussion:
In the small-data regime for the nonlinear equations, the most dangerous term to the
future is the future-ingoing null derivativeLR(+), whereL =∂ t+(vr−c)∂r. All other
derivative-terms in the nonlinear equations, includingLR (+),L R(−), andLR (−),
whereL:=∂ t +(v r +c)∂ r, decay much faster to the future (at an integrable-in-time
rate) and are harmless in the context of global existence. They are also harmless
in the context of shock formation in that they don’t blow up. Similarly, the most
dangerous term to the past is the past-ingoing null derivativeLR (−).

1.3.2.Nonlinear geometric optics.In proving Theorems 1.1 and 1.3, we fundamentally rely on
nonlinear geometric optics, which we implement with the help of an outgoing eikonal functionu,
that is, fort≥0, a solutionuto:

Note that sinceLt= 1 andLu= 0, we haveL= ∂

uthere. That is not important in the sense that the fluid exhibits a lot of time-decay near the
symmetry axis{r= 0}, and hence the fluid is easy to control there directly with respect to the (t, r)
coordinates. We choose different data foruin the two theorems. The details are not important
for the present discussion, but in each theorem,ubehaves liket−rplus correction terms that can
grow logarithmically in time.
The key advantage of the geometric coordinates is the following collection of ideas, which has
roots from many places, notably papers of Alinhac [7, 8], Christodoulou [16], and second author
[32,40,41]:
Relative to the (t, u) coordinates,R ± satisfy a system of wave equations in which
the nonlinear termsdosatisfy the null condition, i.e., no resonant terms are present
in the equations; see Prop. 4.4. Consequently, in (t, u) coordinates, one can study
the dynamics using techniques developed for more standard long-time existence
problems. Moreover, if a shock forms in the solution with respect to the original (t, r)
coordinate system, this must be due to a degeneracy between the two coordinate
systems.
The surprising fact that the null condition holds in (t, u) coordinates doesnotcontradict the fact
that in the original (t, r) coordinate system, the null condition fails, i.e., that there are resonant
16

[PDF page 17]
Riccati terms in the original equationL(rL R(+))∼r(L R(+))2 +· · ·. We will now explain why
there is no contradiction.
When one changes variables from (t, r) to (t, u) coordinates, this introduces a change of variables
factor ∂
∂u r. One can show that schematically, we have:
∂
∂u r∼ −µ,(12)
whereµ>0, theinverse foliation density of the characteristics, satisfies:
µ= 2
Lu .(13)
Geometrically,µ= 0 means that the level sets ofuhave become infinitely dense, signifying shock
formation, whileµgrowing large signifies the separation of the characteristics (rarefaction). When
one changes variables to geometric coordinates in the wave equation forR (+), this generates a
change-of-variables term of the formLµ·L R(+). The key point is thatµsatisfies an evolution
equation of the following schematic form (see (50)):
Lµ∼ −µL R(+) +· · ·,(14)
and it turns out that the change-of-variables termLµ·L R(+) leads to thecomplete cancellation of
the dangerous Riccati termr(L R(+))2 +· · ·in the wave equation forR (+); see (53a). The upshot
is the following:
In all our theorems, relative to the (t, u) coordinates, the nonlinear terms satisfy
the null condition, and the corresponding nonlinear solution behaves like the linear
solutions from Sect. 1.3.1, where for positive times, the role oft−ris played byu.
Hence, relative to the (t, u) coordinates, we can approach the PDE analysis like a small-data global
existence problem for a wave system that satisfies the null condition, the new difficulty being that
the data have a 1/rtail, which makes some of the decay-in-urates worse compared to the case of
compactly supported data. We must also controlµby carefully studying the transport equation
(14).
In the shock-formation/MGHD problem, to estimateµ, we first use the wave equation forR (+)
and our data assumptions to prove a bound of the formµL R(+) ∼ f(u)
t for some smooth function
f >0 (see (95a) for the details); this bound relies on sign of the radiation field, as discussed
in Sect. 1.3.1. We then insert this bound into equation (14) and integrate in time in the (t, u)
coordinate system (recalling thatL= ∂
∂t ), thereby concluding (thanks to the non-integrability int
of 1
t near∞) thatµvanishes in finite time along the singular boundary curveB Future in Fig. 5. Note
that sinceµL R(+) ∼ f(u)
t , the vanishing ofµcauses the blowup ofL R(+), i.e., blowup of a first
derivative ofR (+) in the (t, r) differential structure. On the other hand, simple calculations yield
that in (t, u) coordinates, we haveµL =µ ∂
∂t + 2 ∂
∂u (see Lemma 3.5), and henceµ ∂
∂t R(+) + 2 ∂
∂u R(+)
remains bounded. Since ∂
∂t R(+) also remains bounded (see (118)), it follows that relative to the
geometric coordinates, no first derivative ofR (+) blows up, even whenµvanishes. Our estimates
(see Props. 7.4 and 10.7) also show thatno first derivative ofR (−) blows up to the future, even in
(t, r)coordinates; see (32a)–(32b), (118), and (120).
For the global existence problem, the analysis proceeds in a similar fashion. The main difference
is that the radiation field has the opposite sign, and thus the evolution equation (14) leads to
logarithmic-in-time growth ofµ; see (425a). Hence, the outgoing characteristics separate, and no
singularity ever forms.

1.4.The spherically symmetric isentropic compressible Euler equations.In this section,
we provide a more detailed introduction to the equations. The isentropic Euler equations in three
spatial dimensions can be expressed as a quasilinear hyperbolic system for thevelocityv:R 1+3 →
R3 and thedensityϱ:R 1+3 →R ≥0 of a compressible fluid. In the standard Cartesian differential
structure ofR 1+3 with coordinates (t, x) = (t, x1, x2, x3), the equations of motion are: 19

system of because there are not enough equations. To close the system, we assume thatp=p(ϱ) is
given as a function ofϱ, which is called the equation of state. Our results holdfor anyC 4 equation
of state except for that of the Chaplygin gas, provided that thespeed of soundcis positive whenever
ϱis positive:

In this article, to avoid fluid-vacuum free boundaries, we study only solutions with strictly
positive density. More precisely, we will study perturbations of “background solutions” in which
the velocity completely vanishes and such that the density is everywhere equal to ¯ϱ, where ¯ϱ >0 is
a constant. By rescaling the time coordinatetby a positive constant if necessary, we can assume

3.Acoustic geometry and nonlinear geometric optics
In this section, we construct the acoustic geometry, i.e, the characteristics corresponding to the
outgoing null vectorfieldL, as well as some other related geometric quantities. This is known as
implementingnonlinear geometric optics. They are essential for our sharp analysis of the gradient-
blowup.
3.1.Construction of the eikonal function.LetT ∗ ≥0 be a fixed time. Later, we will set
T∗ = ˚α−1 for a sufficiently small data-parameter ˚α, which we introduce in Sect. 5.
By definition, the eikonal functionuis the solution to the following transport equation initial
value problem:
Lu= 0, u↾ ΣT∗ ∩{r≥ T∗
2 } =T ∗ −r.(40)
Note that the data foruare posed at timeT ∗ and only in the regionr≥ T∗
2 , which corresponds to
u≤ T∗
2 . This setup has two main advantages:
•uis defined only away fromr= 0, where the equations degenerate due to singular factors
ofr(see (32a)–(32b)).
•The gradient-blowup will occur approximately in a subset of{u≤0}. Thus, whenT ∗ = ˚α−1,
the gradient-blowup occurs well within the region whereuis defined. This is important in
that we fundamentally rely onuto derive the singular boundary, i.e., the entire curve of
gradient-blowup.
3.2.Geometric coordinates and partial derivatives.Most of our analysis, especially the
part concerning gradient-blowup, will take place with respect to the differential structure of the
geometric coordinates, which we now define.
Definition 3.1(Geometric coordinates and partial derivatives).Letube the solution to (40).

which we now rigorously define.

existence results, we will prove thatµis strictly positive in all of spacetime.

Remark 3.8(Notational convention regarding the two different coordinate systems).As we ex-
plained in Sect. 1.3.2 and Sect. 3.1, we carry out our PDE analysis in geometric coordinates (t, u)
only in exterior regions, i.e., regions away from{r= 0}; see Def. 6.1. In contrast, we carry out
our PDE analysis in interior regions containing{r= 0}in the standard spherical coordinate (t, r);
see Def. 10.4. As a matter of convenience, in most of the article, we donotmake any notational
distinction for functions (such asR (+)) defined on exterior regions in geometric coordinates versus
interior regions in spherical coordinates. That is, we denoteR (+)(t, u) for the former andR (+)(t, r)
23

[PDF page 24]
for the latter, or even use notation without reference to any coordinate system, such asR (+). This
is consistent with the conventions of differential geometry. Similar statements hold forR (−), as
well other geometric objects that we use, i.e., we denoteL= ∂
∂t as well asL=∂ t + (vr +c)∂ r. This
is not an issue away from the MGHD boundary because the two coordinate systems are related by
the change of variables Υ(t, u) = (t, r) that is shown (in Prop. 9.1) to be as smooth as the solution
away from the MGHD boundary.
One exception is that in certain parts of Prop. 9.1, we prove results in an exterior region in (t, u)-
space that contains part of the MGHD boundary, as well as other results concerning the behavior
of the solution in (t, r)-coordinates on the corresponding image of the region under Υ, and the two
coordinate systems are continuously – but not smoothly – related on the MGHD boundary. Hence,
different conclusions hold in the two coordinate systems when the MGHD boundary is taken into
account (see, e.g., Points 2, 5, and 8 in Prop. 9.1), and there we use notation such asR (+) ◦Υ −1,
R(−) ◦Υ −1 to explicitly indicate that we are viewing the Riemann invariants as a function of (t, r).

Failure of the null condition near the background solution (R (+),R (−)) = (0,0) is equivalent to

σ>0.(52)

equations of Prop. 4.4 have the following key structural property (which is a version of the null
condition): the dangerous quadratic term ( ˘LR(+))2 does not appear on the RHS of either of the
wave equations.

5.Data and conventions for constants
Our main goal in this section is to construct the open sets of Riemann invariant initial data for
which our main MGHD existence and uniqueness results hold. We first introduce our conventions
for explicit and implicit constants; we will use them throughout the paper in our PDE analysis.
23To treat the caseσ<0, we could just change the sign of the parameter ˚αfeatured below in our assumptions on
the data.
25

[PDF page 26]
5.1.Conventions for constants.
•Explicit constantsC >0 are allowed to vary from line to line. They are allowed to depend
on the equation of state, but can be chosen to beindependentof the amplitude of the data
˚α(see (60) and the bootstrap parameterε(see (87),(200),(331)), provided that ˚αandεare
sufficiently small.
•ByA≲B, we mean that there exists aC >0 satisfying the properties above such that
A≤CB.
•ByA≈B, we mean thatA≲BandB≲A.
•ByA=O(B), we mean that there exists aC >0 with the properties above such that
|A| ≤CB.
•For anyα<1,α 1+
denotes any quantityQsuch that there is a numberδ>0, independent
of all sufficiently smallα, with|Q| ≤α 1+δ.
•We will also use the following standard “little o” notation:
o(˚α) (57)
to denote any ˚α-dependent quantityQsuch|Q| →0 as ˚α↓0.

˚R(+)(r) :=R (+)(0, r), ˚R(−)(r) :=R (−)(0, r).(58)

Remark 5.1(Specific profile only for convenience).It is only for convenience/ to help guide the
reader that we have focused on the background (60). Our main results can be extended to a much
larger class of background “profiles” that enjoy suitable properties. We outline the extension in
Appendix A.
Our analysis will show that the minus sign on RHS (60) is fundamental for ensuring thatL R(+)
blows up in finite time. Note that the background satisfies the following estimates:

that the data is in fact asymptotically flat in a strong sense. (61b) is fundamentally important for
our analysis and is not implied by (61a).
In our main MGHD theorem, we will consider data that are a perturbation ofA, where the
undifferentiated perturbation has a faster decay rate at infinity compared to the background, and

Discussion of proof.For the main ideas on how to prove the lemma, we refer the reader to the proofs
of Props. 11.6, 7.4, 10.7, and 11.8 where similar but much more difficult estimates are derived. Those
propositions are much more difficult to prove because there we have to control the solution up to
its classical lifespan, which is of size≳exp( C
˚α ) in the case of Props. 11.6 and 7.4, and infinite in the
case of Props. 10.7, and 11.8. In contrast, for Lemma 5.4, we only need to control the solution for
T∗ = 1
˚α amounts of time, a tiny portion of the lifespan in which the nonlinearities are negligible.
Finally, we remark that as before, (28a)–(28b) allow us to replacecwith 1 in the estimates up to
harmless error terms. □
Remark 5.5.Lemma 5.4 could also be proved by introducing a fluid potential, which would satisfy
a nonlinear wave equation, and then deriving weighted energy estimates for the wave equation using
the method of commuting vectorfields. Such a proof would require different (i.e., Sobolev-type)
regularity assumptions on the data.

6.Bootstrap assumptions on a globally hyperbolic exterior region
6.1.Globally hyperbolic exterior bootstrap region.We will prove our main Exterior Region
results with the help of a bootstrap argument on globally hyperbolic subsets of spacetime. The
next definition captures the basic structure of the globally hyperbolic subsets that we encounter in
our analysis. See the proof of Prop. 9.1 for the specific globally hyperbolic subsets that play a role
in our bootstrap argument.
Definition 6.1(Globally hyperbolic exterior bootstrap region).A subsetGH Ext
Boot of spacetime is
said to be aglobally hyperbolic exterior bootstrap regionif:
GHExt
Boot ⊂ {t≥T ∗} ∩ {u≤T ∗/2},(86)
and if for every pointq∈GH Ext
Boot, the past-directed integral curves ofLandL emanating fromqre-
main in the interior ofGHExt
Boot until they intersect a pointq′ satisfyingq ′ ∈GH Ext
Boot
T (ΣT∗ ∩ {u≤T ∗/2}).
Remark 6.2(Connection to the standard notion of global hyperbolicity).Our definitions of
“globally hyperbolic” are not standard but can be shown to be equivalent to standard ones from
Lorentzian geometry. We have adopted our definitions because they are simple to state and suf-
ficient for our PDE analysis. In Appendix B, we prove that our maximal globally hyperbolic
development is globally hyperbolic in the standard sense of Lorentzian geometry; see Prop. B.14.
6.2.Bootstrap assumptions on GH Ext
Boot.As is standard for evolution quasilinear problems, we
find it convenient to derive estimates via a bootstrap argument. To this end, we fix a globally
hyperbolic exterior bootstrap regionGH Ext
Boot, and we assume that the following inequalities hold
for some numberεsatisfying:

Remark 7.2(Sign of the radiation field).LHS (94a) is the future-radiation field of the solution.
Note that it is everywherepositiveand decays away from the wave zone. This is the main mechanism
driving our shock-forming MGHD results. Similar results also hold in the context of our global
existence result, Theorem 11.3. Specifically, LHS (408) is the future-radiation field, and it is
everywherenegative, leading to global compression.

Remark 7.5(Strict improvement of the bootstrap assumptions, except possiblyµ>0).Recall
that we assumed only that 0< ε≤ ˚α3/4 in the bootstrap assumptions onGH Ext
Boot that we made
in Section 6; see (87). It is therefore easy to see that when ˚αis sufficiently small, the estimates
of Props. 7.1 and 7.4 collectively imply strict improvements of the bootstrap assumptions, except
it is possible thatµvanishes on the closure ofGH Ext
Boot. In the two propositions imply that the
ε-involving bootstrap assumptions in fact hold withC ˚αin place ofε, which is a strict improvement
ifε:= ˚α3/4 andC ˚α≤ ˚α3/4. Until Sect. 11, we will often silently use this fact in the following
manner: all of theε-involving bootstrap assumptions we have made and the estimates we have
proved hold onGH Ext
Boot withεreplaced byC ˚α.

8.Diffeomorphism and homeomorphism properties of the change of variables map
In this section, we prove a proposition that exhibits various diffeomorphism and homeomorphism
properties of the change of variables map Υ from Sect. 3.5. When proving our main MGHD theorem,
we will use the proposition to transition back and forth between the two coordinate systems.
Proposition 8.1(Diffeomorphism and homeomorphism properties of the change of variables map).
LetGH Ext
Boot be a globally hyperbolic exterior bootstrap region in the sense of Def. 6.1 that, in addi-
tion, is foliated by intervals of constantuand has a piecewiseC 1 future-boundary i.e., either:
GHExt
Boot =∪ t∈[T∗,TBoot){(t, u)|A(t)< u < B(t)},(131)
40

[PDF page 41]
or:
GHExt
Boot =∪ t∈[T∗,TBoot){(t, u)| − ∞< u < B(t)},(132)

Proof.We assume that GHExt

Next, using (48) and the bootstrap assumptions, we find that:
det(dΥ(t, u)) =−cµ<0,onGH Ext
Boot.(135)
Hence, by the inverse function theorem, Υ is a local diffeomorphism onGH Ext
Boot. Moreover, since
(48) implies:
∂
∂u r=−cµ,(136)
it follows that at each fixedt∈[T ∗, TBoot), the mapu→r(t, u) is one-to-one foru∈(A(t), B(t))
and thusu→r(t, u) extends to aC 3, one-to-one function ofuon the closed interval [(A(t), B(t)].
It follows that the extended map Υ is injective on∪ t∈[T∗,TBoot){(t, u)|A(t)≤u≤B(t)}. The main
remaining task is to show that the extended functionr(t, u) is such that the functionu→r(T Boot, u)
is one-to-one foru∈[A(T Boot), B(TBoot)]. This implies that Υ is injective on GHExt
Boot, which, by
compactness, implies that Υ is a homeomorphism on GHExt
Boot.
To show thatu→r(T Boot, u) is injective foru∈[A(T Boot), B(TBoot)], it suffices to prove the
following claim: thatµ(T Boot, u)>0 must hold for allu∈[A(T Boot), B(TBoot)] except at possibly
one pointu ∗. The injectiveness of the mapu→r(T Boot, u) then follows from the identity (136)
(which by continuity continues to hold at timeT Boot) and the mean value theorem.
To prove the claim from the previous paragraph, we first restrict our attention to the open interval
(A(TBoot), B(TBoot)). Sinceµis positive onGH Ext
Boot, at any pointu ∗ ∈(A(T Boot), B(TBoot)) at
which the functionu→µ(T Boot, u) vanishes,µachieves its minimum value of 0, i.e.,µ(T Boot, u∗) =
˘Xµ(TBoot, u∗) = 0, ˘X ˘Xµ(TBoot, u∗)≥0. Theµ-estimates of Prop. 7.1 imply that these three
conditions are simultaneously possible only for at most a single pointu ∗ satisfyingu ∗ =o( ˚α),
at which we must have ˘X ˘Xµ(TBoot, u∗)> 3
2. That is, the mapu→µ(T Boot, u) can have at
most a single 0 at an interior pointu ∗ ∈(A(T Boot), B(TBoot)). Moreover, ifu→µ(T Boot, u) does
have such an interior zero atu ∗, then theµ-estimates of Prop. 7.1 imply that ˘Xµ(TBoot, u)>0
for all pointsu > u ∗ satisfyingµ(T Boot, u)<1/200 and ˘Xµ(TBoot, u)<0 for all pointsu < u ∗
41

[PDF page 42]
satisfyingµ(T Boot, u)<1/200. This implies, in particular, that eitherµ(T Boot, A(TBoot))≥1/200
or ˘Xµ(TBoot, A(TBoot))>0. That is, eitherµ(T Boot, A(TBoot))≥1/200, orµ(T Boot, A(TBoot)) is
more positive than nearby pointsµ(T Boot, u) withu∈(A(T Boot), B(TBoot)). Either way, we must
haveµ(T Boot, A(TBoot))>0. Similar logic implies thatµ(T Boot, B(TBoot))>0. We have therefore
shown that ifu→µ(T Boot, u) has a zero on (A(T Boot), B(TBoot)), then it must be the only such
zero, andµ(T Boot, A(TBoot))>0,µ(T Boot, B(TBoot))>0.
To finish the proof of the claim, we will show via a contradiction argument that on the closed in-
terval [A(TBoot), B(TBoot)], it is impossible foru→µ(T Boot, u) to vanish at both endpointsA(T Boot)
andB(T Boot) while being positive in the interior. If this were to occur, thenµ(T Boot, A(TBoot)) = 0,
˘Xµ(TBoot, A(TBoot))≥0,µ(T Boot, B(TBoot)) = 0, and ˘Xµ(TBoot, B(TBoot))≤0, and the esti-
mates of Prop. 7.1 imply thatA(T Boot)≥ −|o( ˚α)|andB(T Boot)≤ |o( ˚α)|. Since we also have
A(TBoot)< B(T Boot), it follows that [A(T Boot), B(TBoot)]⊂[−|o( ˚α)|,|o(˚α)|], and the estimates
of Prop. 7.1 then imply that 3
2 < ˘X ˘Xµ(TBoot, u)<3 for allu∈[A(T Boot), B(TBoot)]. That is,
u→ ˘Xµ(TBoot, u) is strictly increasing on [A(T Boot), B(TBoot)], making it impossible to have both
˘Xµ(TBoot, A(TBoot))≥0 and ˘Xµ(TBoot, B(TBoot))≤0. We have therefore demonstrated a contra-
diction. In total, we have proved the claim from the previous paragraph, which completes the proof
of the proposition.
□

The structure of the Cauchy horizon and the parameterization of the boundary . Note
that as subsets of spacetime, the integral curves of ˘L =µL andL are the same, and that ˘L =
µ ∂
∂t + 2 ∂
∂u isC 2 with respect to (t, u) on the closure ofGH UMin. Hence, by construction in the
previous paragraph, the right boundary of the closure ofGH UMin is a portion of the integral curve
of ˘L that emanates from the point with geometric coordinates (T Crease, UMin), andµmust vanish
somewhere on this right boundary. Let us denote this integral curve portion by ˘γ(Min).µcannot
vanish at the upper endpoint (t(U Min), T∗/2) of ˘γ(Min), for otherwise, theµ-estimates of Prop. 7.1
would imply that ˘Lµis strictly positive at (t(U Min), T∗/2), and thusµwould turn strictly negative
along ˘γ(Min) to the past of (t(U Min), T∗/2). By continuity, this would imply that there is a number
U > U Min such thatµvanishes inGH U, a contradiction.µcannot vanish at the lower endpoint
(T∗,u min(T∗)) of ˘γ(Min), since this point lies in the data hypersurface, whereµ≈1. Hence, the zero
ofµalong ˘γ(Min) must occur at an interior pointqof the integral curve. Let us denote the geometric
coordinates ofqby (t ′, u′). Thenµmust have a minimum atq, i.e.,µ(t ′, u′) = ˘Lµ(t′, u′) = 0, and
˘L˘Lµ(t′, u′)≥0. Moreover, we must havet ′ ≥T Crease andu ′ ≥U Crease since otherwise, we would
haveq∈GH Singular;Ext, which is impossible becauseµ>0 holds everywhere inGH Singular;Ext (µ

Proof thatΥ(B Future)∪Υ(C T∗/2)has a corner . Recall that∂ −BFuture ∈ BFuture and that∂ −BFuture
is a past limit point ofC T∗/2, i.e., its closure satisfies CT∗/2 =C T∗/2 ∪ {∂−BFuture}. Earlier in the
proof, we showed that the tangent vector to the curve Υ(B Future) at Υ(∂−BFuture) is proportional to
L↾ Υ(∂−BFuture), and that the tangent vector to the curve Υ( CT∗/2) at Υ(∂−BFuture) is proportional to
L ↾Υ(∂−BFuture). The formulas (29) and the estimates of Prop. 7.4 together imply thatLandL are
everywhere transversal, which shows that the curve Υ(BFuture)∪Υ(C T∗/2) has a corner at∂ −BFuture.
Global hyperbolicity. Finally, we show that the regionMGHD Ext from (143) is globally hyper-
bolic in the sense of Definition 6.1. We have shown thatMGHD Ext is foliated by integral curves of
L(i.e., portions of level sets ofu) that connect points in Σ T∗ to points on its future-boundary. More-
over, given any past-directed integral curve ofL emanating from a point inMGHD Ext, bothtand
udecrease towards Σ T∗ (sinceL t= 1 and ˘Lu= 2). By ODE uniqueness, such an integral curve can
never intersect the Cauchy horizon boundaryC T∗/2 (which does not belong toMGHD Ext), which
is itself an integral curve of ˘L where the solution is smooth in both coordinate systems. Hence,
this integral curve terminates along Σ T∗ at someu-value that is more negative than its starting
value. □
10.The MGHD existence and uniqueness theorem for shock-forming solutions,
including the Interior Region
In this section, we prove our main theorem on the existence, uniqueness, and stability of the
MGHD for open sets of data that lead to shock-forming solutions. The most difficult aspects of
the theorem concern the Exterior Region, which we already treated in Prop. 9.1. Hence, most of
the effort in this section is dedicated towards controlling the solution in the Interior Region. To
that end, we will exploit the decay of the solution with respect tot−rin the interior; despite the
failure of the null condition in the equations, the decay int−rwill allow us to control the solution
for an amount of timeT Max that is sufficient for capturing the entire MGHD.
10.1.Statement of the MGHD existence and uniqueness theorem.In this section, we
state our main theorem on MGHD existence and uniqueness. We provide the proof in Sect. 10.6.
The full statement of the theorem refers to theacoustical metriconR 1+3, which is the following
Lorentzian metric:
g:=−dt⊗dt+c −2
3X
a=1
(dxa −v adt)⊗(dx a −v adt).(184)
The tensorgcan be used to construct the characteristics. More precisely, for the 3Dcom-
pressible Euler equations, the characteristic subset in co-tangent space includes the sound cones
{ξ|(g −1)αβξαξβ = 0}. The causal structure of the solution and the correct intrinsic notion of
global hyperbolicity are tied tog. We refer to Appendix B for further discussion.

map from geometric coordinates to(t, r)coordinates. In particular,M Future is the union of three
sub-regions depicted in Fig.5: i) the “flat” region in betweenΣ 0 andΣ T∗. ii) the Interior Region,
which contains some points lying to the future ofΣ T∗; and iii) the Exterior Region, which contains
other points lying to the future ofΣ T∗ and which is precisely the region where the eikonal function
uis defined. 26 The intersection of the Interior and Exterior Regions is equal to the portion of the
characteristic{u= T∗
2 }depicted in the figure.

Under the data assumptions stated in Section 5 (see Appendix A for a much larger class of data
to which our results can be extended), if ˚αis sufficiently small, then the following results hold,
whereT ∗ = 1
˚α.
(1)(Existence and uniqueness of the future-solution). The data onΣ 0 launch a unique
classical solution(R (+),R (−))to equations(32a)–(32b)onM Future that satisfies the bound-
ary conditions(33)–(34).
(2)(Estimates). The state of the solution at the “early” timeT ∗ = 1
˚α is provided by Lemma 5.4.
For later times, the solution satisfies the Exterior Region estimates 27 of Prop. 7.4 and the
Interior Region estimates featured in Prop. 10.7.
(3)(Structure of the future-boundary). The future-boundary ofM Future is the union of
two curves which, by definition, do not belong toM Future. The first curve is the future-
singular boundaryΥ(B Future), which is closed, and which was constructed in Prop. 9.1. The
past-boundary ofΥ(B Future)is a single point, the crease, denoted byΥ(∂ −BFuture)in Fig.5.
The second curve is the Cauchy horizonC Max
Future, an integral curve ofL that does not contain
∂−BFuture but features it as a past-endpoint. Moreover,C Max
Future terminates to the future at
a point(T Max,0)on the axis of symmetry satisfying the following estimate, whereT Crease is
the value oftat the crease (see(138)):
TMax ≤2T Crease +O(1).(185)
(4)(Behavior of the solution on the future-boundary). As was shown in Prop. 9.1, the
solution does not extend classically toΥ(B Future)because|∂ rR(+)(q)| → ∞asqapproaches
(from the Exterior Region) any point onΥ(B Future). In contrast, the solution uniquely
extends as aC 2 solution28 toC max, i.e., the solution exists classically up to the Cauchy
horizon (which does not contain its past-endpointΥ(∂ −BFuture)).
(5)(Global hyperbolicity).M Future is globally hyperbolic and contains the Cauchy hypersur-
faceΣ 0. That is, it satisfies the following:
•For every pointq∈M Future, the past-directed integral curve ofL emanating fromq
remains in the interior ofM Future until it intersectsΣ 0.
•For every pointq∈M Future, the past-directed integral curve ofLemanating from
qremains in the interior ofM Future until it intersects either the axis of symmetry
{r= 0}orΣ 0.
(6)(Similar results to the past). Fort≤0, the solution obeys analogous results, i.e., there
exists a unique classical solution on a globally hyperbolic setM Past contained in{t≤0},
the past-boundary ofM Past is the union of a closed, unbounded past-singular boundaryB Past
that emanates from the past-crease∂ +BPast and a pre-compact past-Cauchy horizonC Max
Past
that emanates from∂ +BPast and terminates at the axis of symmetry. Moreover, the solution
obeys estimates corresponding to Props. 7.4 and 10.7 with the following three changes: i)
replacingtwith−t=|t|; ii) interchanging the roles ofLandL ; iii) interchangingR (+)
andR (−). In particular, withB Past denoting the past singular boundary,|∂ rR(−)|blows up
asΥ(B Past)is approached from any sequence of points lying in the past Interior Region.

28(R(+),R (−)) could actually be shown to beC 3, aside from the issue that there would be anr-weight in front
of the third-order derivatives. In fact, in the Exterior Region estimates of Prop. 7.4, we proved that the solution is
C3. In the Interior Region estimates of Prop. 10.7, we only derived estimates for the up-to-second order derivatives of
(R(+),R (−)); we did not try to control the third derivatives because we did not need such control to close the proof.

MoreoverM Past is globally hyperbolic in the sense of Point 5, but with the roles of past and
future interchanged and with the roles ofLandL interchanged.
(7)(Global hyperbolicity ofM Max -PartA). LetM Max denote the unionM Future ∪M Past,
viewed as a subset of(t, r)coordinate space. ThenM Max is globally hyperbolic withΣ 0 as a
Cauchy hypersurface. That is, it satisfies the following:
•For every pointq∈M Max, the maximally extended integral curve ofLpassing through
qintersects eitherΣ 0 or{r= 0}.
•For every pointq ′ ∈M Max, the maximally extended integral curve ofL passing through
qintersects eitherΣ 0 or{r= 0}.
(8)(Global hyperbolicity ofM Max -PartB). If we now viewM Max to be a spherically
symmetric subset 29 ofR 1+3, then withgdenoting the acoustical metric onR 1+3 defined in
(184),M Max is an open set, and the Lorentzian manifold(M Max,g)is globally hyperbolic
in the “standard” sense of Lorentzian geometry (see Def. B.4), where{t= 0}is a Cauchy
hypersurface.
(9)(Maximality ofM Max) LetM Max be as in the previous point, in particular viewed as
a spherically symmetric subset ofR 1+3. Let(R (+),R (−))be the corresponding classical
solution onM Max guaranteed by the above points. Assume that fMand(eϱ,ev1,ev2,ev3)satisfy
the following conditions:
•M Max ⊂fM⊂R 1+3.
•(eϱ,ev1,ev2,ev3)is aC 2 solution to the compressible Euler equations(15a)–(15b)on
fMthat agrees with the spherically symmetric solution onM Max constructed above.
Note that while onM Max,(eϱ,ev1,ev2,ev3)is spherically symmetric and has correspond-
ing spherical Riemann invariants( eR+, eR−)satisfying( eR+, eR−)↾ fM= (R (+),R (−)),
(eϱ,ev1,ev2,ev3)is not assumed to be spherically symmetric on fM\MMax, nor is the re-
gion fM\MMax assumed to be spherically symmetric.
•Let egdenote the acoustical metric(184), evaluated at the solution(eϱ,ev 1,ev2,ev3). We
assume that fMis open and that the Lorentzian manifold( fM,eg)is globally hyperbolic
in the sense of Def. B.4 and contains{t= 0}as a Cauchy hypersurface.
Then fM=M Max. That is, there is no globally hyperbolic development of the data that
strictly containsM Max.
(10)(Uniqueness of the MGHD) Let(R (+),R (−))be the classical solution onM Max guaran-
teed by the above points. Assume that cMand(bϱ,bv1,bv2,bv3)satisfy the following conditions:
• {t= 0} ⊂ cM⊂R 1+3.
•(bϱ,bv1,bv2,bv3)is aC 2 solution to the compressible Euler equations(15a)–(15b)on cM
that has the same initial data 30 on{t= 0}as(R (+),R (−)).
•Let bgdenote the acoustical metric(184), evaluated at the solution(bϱ,bv 1,bv2,bv3). We
assume that cMis open, and the Lorentzian manifold( cM,bg)is globally hyperbolic in
the sense of Def. B.4, and contains{t= 0}as a Cauchy hypersurface. Note that we
are not assuming that(bϱ,bv1,bv2,bv3)or cMare spherically symmetric.
ThencM⊂M Max,(bϱ,bv1,bv2,bv3)is spherically symmetric on cMwith corresponding spherical
Riemann invariants( bR+, bR−), and(R (+),R (−))↾ cM= (bR+, bR−). That is,(M Max,(R (+),R (−)))
29In Appendix B, the corresponding set inR 1+3 is precisely described asS(M Max ×S2), whereSis the map defined
in (452).
30By “same initial data,” we mean that the data for (bϱ,bv 1,bv2,bv3) are the unique spherically symmetric data
corresponding to (R (+),R (−))↾ {t=0}.
57

[PDF page 58]
is an extension of all other globally hyperbolic developments (see Def. B.7) of the data on
{t= 0}and thus is the unique MGHD of the data.
Remark 10.2(Carefully distinguish maximality from uniqueness).Point 9 says that (MMax,(R (+),R (−)))
is a largest possible globally hyperbolic classical solution, i.e., maximality. A subtlety is that Point 9
by itself does not preclude the existence of a distinct maximal GHD fMMax ̸=M Max satisfying the
same assumptions and conclusions of Point 9. However, Point 10 rules out the possibility of a dis-
tinctfMMax because (MMax,(R (+),R (−)))containsall other globally hyperbolic classical solutions;
this “containment” is the key ingredient behind MGHD uniqueness. In contrast, it is known that
for a different hyperbolic PDE, there existC ∞ data such that no solution exists with properties
as in Point 10, even though multiple distinct solutions with properties as in Point 9 do exist; see
Appendix B.
