Remark 10.8(Strict improvement of the Interior bootstrap assumptions).Recall that we assumed
only that 0< ε≤ ˚α3/4 in our bootstrap assumptions onGH INT
Boot (see (200)). It is therefore easy
to see that when ˚αis sufficiently small, the estimates of Prop. 10.7 yield strict improvements of all
the bootstrap assumptions.
Remark 10.9.As in Remarks 7.6 and 11.7, some of the estimates in Prop. 10.7 could be improved,
but we don’t need such improvements to close our bootstrap argument. For example, in the Interior
Region, the linear Riemann invariants could be shown to satisfy:

10.6.Proof of Theorem 10.1.
Opening Remarks: It is a standard result that a unique classical solution exists in the “small-
time” region in between Σ 0 and Σ T∗, which, when ˚αis small, is a tiny portion of the classical
lifespan, during which the nonlinearities are negligible. We refer readers to Sect. 11 for detailed
proofs of how to control the solution for 0≤t≤T ∗, where the data in that section has the opposite
sign, but the same strategy for controlling the solution in the small-time region could be applied;
we omit the details here.
Next, we recall that in Prop. 9.1, we constructed the solution in the Exterior Region and in
particular proved the blowup of|∂ rR(+)|on Υ(B Future) and of|∂ rR(−)|on Υ(B Past); see Fig. 5.
Therefore, to prove Theorem 10.1, the main remaining tasks we have to accomplish are to construct
the solution in the Interior Region and to prove uniqueness of the solution and the maximality and
uniqueness of the MGHD.
Throughout this proof,T CH denotes the function yielded by Prop. 9.1.
As a final opening remark, we remind the reader that for almost all results in the theorem, it
suffices to understand the solution for timest≥0, i.e., to understand the solution onM Future. The
past-dynamics are almost a mirror image of the future-dynamics; see Remark 1.5.
Separation of the Interior Region into two pieces : In Fig. 6, we have divided the Interior
Region into two subsets,IandII, separated by the constant-time hypersurface portion Σ TCH( T∗
2 ) ∩
{r≤R(T CH( T∗

classical existence inI∪II, i.e., onM Future.

hyperbolic with Cauchy hypersurface Σ T∗ in the sense of Point 5 of the theorem; it is a standard
result that the remaining “small-time” region in between Σ 0 and Σ T∗ is globally hyperbolic with
Cauchy hypersurface Σ0, which, when combined with the global hyperbolicity ofM Future ∩{t≥T ∗},
yields the desired global hyperbolicity ofM Future with Cauchy hypersurface Σ 0.
To prove the global hyperbolicity ofM Future ∩ {t≥T ∗}, we start by recalling that we already
analyzed the exterior region in the proof of Prop. 9.1. Hence, it suffices to consider the Interior
Region. Letqbe a point in the Interior Region that is disjoint from the Cauchy horizon and
disjoint from the characteristic curve{u= T∗
2 }, which separates the Interior Region from the
Exterior Region.
Consider the backwards integral curvet→γ q(t) ofL emanating fromq. SinceL t= 1 and
since (28a)–(28b), (29), and Prop. 10.7 imply thatL r=v r −c=−1 + O(˚α)
1+t+r, it follows thatt

Uniqueness of the classical solution on M Future: This is a standard result, whose proof we
briefly describe. Let ( bR+, bR−) be anotherC 2 solution to equations (32a)–(32b) onM Future with
the same initial data on Σ 0 as (R(+),R (−)). Let (R ∆

Putting some results together : Also recalling Prop. 9.1, we see that we have proved Points 1-5
in the theorem. As we described in the opening remarks, Point 6 follows from similar considerations.
Proof that M Max is globally hyperbolic, PartA : Point 7 in the theorem follows easily from
the global hyperbolicity ofM Future andM Past.
Proof that M Max is globally hyperbolic, PartB : Point 8 follows from Point 7 and standard
arguments in Lorentzian geometry. For the reader’s convenience, we provide a proof in Prop. B.14,
of Appendix B, after providing some background material on Lorentzian geometry.
Maximality of M Max: We now prove Point 9 stated in the theorem. We argue by contradiction.
Suppose that there exists a fMwith the stated properties but withM Max ⊊fM. Since fMequipped
with the acoustical metricg(see (184)) is assumed to be globally hyperbolic in the sense 32 of
Def. B.4 with{t= 0}as a Cauchy hypersurface, Lemma B.5 (see also Remark B.6) guarantees that
all inextendible causal curves in (fM,g) must intersect{t= 0}.
Note that fMand the solution on it are not assumed to be spherically symmetric in fM\MMax.
However, we will now prove thatfMand the solution on itarespherically symmetric. By [10], fMis
foliated by the level sets of a smooth temporal functionτ(i.e., all level sets ofτareg-spacelike and
the gradient ofτisg-timelike and everywhere non-vanishing) such that{τ= 0}={t= 0}. Since
32This definition only directly refers tog-timelike curves, while Lemma B.5 has implications for any inextendible
g-causal curve.
82

[PDF page 83]
the initial data for (15a)–(15b) are spherically symmetric by assumption, we can use a standard
argument based on energy estimates33 on the level sets ofτto show that the solution is spherically
symmetric on all of fM. By extending the solution if necessary to be independent of angular
coordinates relative to standard spherical coordinates onR 1+3, we can assume that the region fM
is spherically symmetric. Hence, for the rest of the argument, we can use (t, r)-coordinates and
spherical Riemann invariants, and we can viewM Max andfMas subsets of (t, r)-coordinate space.
Letq∈ fM\MMax. Without loss of generality, we will assume thattis positive atq; the case in
whichtis negative atqcan be handled using similar arguments. Lett→γ q(t) be the maximally
extended,t-parameterized integral curve ofL infMthat emanates fromq. Sinceγ q(t) is ag-causal
curve, the aforementioned Lemma B.5 guarantees thatt→γ q(t)mustintersect Σ 0 (at time 0)
astdecreases. Recall that the future-boundary ofM Max, i.e.,C Max
Future ∪Υ(B Future), is the graph of
a continuous function and thatM Max lies below this graph; see Fig. 5. Hence,γ q must intersect
CMax
Future ∪Υ(B Future) astdecreases. We will show that this is impossible.γ q(t) cannot intersect
Υ(BFuture) astdecreases because|∂ rR(+)|blows up as Υ(B Future) is approached withinM Max, i.e.,
the solution cannot be classically continued to Υ(B Future), and thus Υ(BFuture) cannot belong to fM.
Ifγ q(t) intersectsC Max
Future astdecreases, then since the solution (R (+),R (−)) uniquely extends to
beC 2 onC Max
Future, by ODE uniqueness, this integral curve must be a parameterization of the future-
Cauchy horizonC Max
Future. Now before hitting Σ 0,C Max
Future terminates (to the past) on the future-crease
Υ(∂−BFuture), where|∂ rR(+)|blows up. This contradicts the fact thatt→γ q(t) intersects Σ0. We
have therefore shown that no pointq∈ fM\MMax exists.
Uniqueness of M Max: We now prove the final Point 10 stated in the theorem. As we noted
above, the MGHD manifoldM Max that we constructed has the following key structural prop-
erty: its future-boundaryC Max
Future ∪Υ(B Future), viewed as a spherically symmetric subset ofR 1+3,
is the graph of a continuous function (see Point 8 of Prop. 9.1), and similar remarks apply to its
past-boundary. The “uniqueness” conclusions stated in the final point of Theorem 10.1 therefore
follow from [19, Theorem 4.82], modulo the following remark: While strictly speaking, the re-
sults of [19, Theorem 4.82] were derived for scalar quasilinear wave equations onR 1+3 of the form
(g−1)αβ(∂ϕ)∂α∂βϕ=N(ϕ, ∂ϕ), withNa nonlinearity, forC 2 solutions, the irrotational and isen-
tropic compressible Euler equations are equivalent to a quasilinear wave equation system, whereϕis
an array comprising the densityϱand the Cartesian velocity componentsv 1, v2, v3; see [32,41]. The
fact that the fluid wave equations from [32,41] are system with a common wave operator, as opposed
to the scalar wave equations treated in [19, Theorem 4.82], does not have any affect on the proof
or conclusions of [19, Theorem 4.82]. For the reader’s convenience, we discuss [19, Theorem 4.82]
in more detail in Sect. B.4.2.
This concludes our proof of Theorem 10.1.
33More precisely, one could commute equations (15a)–(15b) with the standard Euclidean rotation vectorfields. The
initialL 2 energy of the non-radial components of the solution, including the rotationally-differentiated components,
is vanishing in view of the spherical symmetry of the data. Moreover, since the equations are invariant under
the Euclidean rotation symmetry group, the commuted equations are linear in the components of the non-radial
components of the solution, i.e., the components that one would like to show are identically vanishing. Hence, the
desired vanishing of the non-radial components of the solution follows from standardL 2-type energy identities and
Gr¨ onwall’s inequality with respect to the time functionτ.

11.Global existence
In this section, we prove Theorem 11.3, which yields global existence for an open set of small
“rarefactive” initial data that essentially has the opposite sign (see also Remark 11.2) of the “com-
pressive” data from Theorem 10.1.
11.1.Data at time0.As we described in the introduction, to prove global existence in the
“rarefactive regime,” we will consider perturbations of the following profile, whose sign is the
opposite of the “compressive profile” in (60):
B(r) := ˚αarctan(r)

sense:

Remark 11.1(Don’t need the extra derivative for global existence).Note that (306) involves one
fewer derivative compared to (62). The reason is that in Theorem 10.1, the extra derivative was
used only to derive the structure of the singular boundary and Cauchy horizon, which are not
present for the global solutions that we study in Theorem 11.3.

Remark 11.2(Changing the sign for global existence).For many of the estimates in Sect. 11, the
only important difference compared to the rest of the paper is that signed terms involving ˚αhave
the opposite sign, thanks to our assumption (304).
11.2.Statement of the main global existence theorem.In this section, we state our main
global existence theorem. We provide the proof in Sect. 11.10, after deriving the a priori estimates
that lie at the heart of the result.
Theorem 11.3(Main global existence theorem).Consider the compressible Euler equations(32a)–
(32b)under anyC 3 equation of state with positive sound speed, including the Chaplygin gas. With-
out loss of generality, assume the positivity condition 34 (52)on the constantσ, except in the case of
the Chaplygin gas, for whichσ= 0. Assume that the Riemann invariant data functions( ˚R(+), ˚R(−))
satisfy(305a)–(305b)(see Appendix A for a much larger class of data to which our results can be
extended). If ˚αis sufficiently small, then the following results hold.
•(Global existence and uniqueness). The initial data launch a unique classical solution
(R(+),R (−))to the compressible Euler equations(32a)–(32b)that satisfies the boundary
conditions(33)–(34)and exists globally in space and time, i.e., for(t, r)∈(−∞,∞)×[0,∞).
34Ifσ<0, then we can just change the sign of ˚α, and the theorem still holds.
84

[PDF page 85]
•(Future-estimates). The eikonal functionuandµare classical solutions to(316)and
(50)respectively in the away-from-time-axis region(t, r)∈[0,∞)×[1,∞). Moreover, for
t≥0,(R (+),R (−)),u, andµobeys the estimates proved in Props. 11.6 and 11.8.
•(Future-diffeomorphism property of the change of variables map). LetU(t)be the
function constructed in Sect. 11.4 (note that(323)–(324)imply thatU(t)is a perturbation
oft−1). Then the change of variables mapΥ(t, u) = (t, r)is aC 2 diffeomorphism from the
closed set{(t, u) 0≤t <∞, u≤U(t)}onto the closed image set{r≥1} ∩ {0≤t <∞}.
•(Similar results to the past). Finally, fort <0, the solution obeys analogous results,
where the estimates corresponding to Props. 11.6 and 11.8 are obtained by making the follow-
ing three changes: i) replacingtwith−t=|t|; ii) interchangingLandL ; iii) interchanging
R(+) andR (−).

11.5.Bootstrap assumptions and the away-from-time-axis and near-time-axis regions.
To prove global existence, we will make bootstrap assumptions on a spacetime slab for a bootstrap
timeT Boot >0:
S TBoot :={r≥0} ∩ {0≤t < T Boot}.(328)
We divide the bootstrap assumptions and the PDE analysis into the away-from-time-axis region:
S TBoot
Away :={r≥1} ∩ {0≤t < T Boot}={(t, r)|0≤t < T Boot}, u(t, r)∈(−∞,U(t)]},(329)
where the acoustic geometry and (t, u) coordinates are defined, and the near-time-axis region:
S TBoot
Near :={0≤r≤1} ∩ {0≤t < T Boot},(330)
where the solution exhibits a lot of time-decay, enabling us to work only in (t, r) coordinates.

11.7.The main a priori estimates for the Riemann invariants.In the next proposition, we
derive our main a priori estimates for the Riemann invariants. The proof relies on a combination
of methods used in the proofs of Props. 10.7 and 10.7, but modified to account for the fact thatµ
grows logarithmically35 in time and to account for the fact that we are starting the analysis from
time 0, rather than timeT ∗.

of Sect. 11.5, if ˚αis sufficiently small, then the following estimates hold.

11.9.Diffeomorphism properties of the change of variables map.In this section, we prove
a simple lemma showing that the change of variables map from geometric to Cartesian coordinates
is a diffeomorphism in the entire bootstrap region.
Lemma 11.10(Diffeomorphism properties of the change of variables map).Assume that the
bootstrap assumptions of Sect. 11.5 hold on the open-at-the-top regionS TBoot
Away ={r≥1} ∩ {0≤t <
TBoot}defined in(329). Then if ˚αis sufficiently small andT Boot <∞, the change of variables map
Υ(t, u) := (t, r)extends to a globalC 2 diffeomorphism from the closed set{(t, u) 0≤t≤T Boot, u≤
U(t)}(whereU(t)is defined in Sect. 11.4) onto the closed set S TBoot
Away ={r≥1} ∩ {0≤t≤T Boot}.
Similarly, ifT Boot =∞, thenΥis aC 2 diffeomorphism from the closed set{(t, u) 0≤t <
∞, u≤U(t)}onto the closed image set{r≥1} ∩ {0≤t <∞}.
Proof.We will give the proof only in the caseT Boot <∞since the caseT Boot =∞can be handled
with similar but simpler arguments.
First, Υ extends to aC 2 function on the domain closure{(t, u) 0≤t≤T Boot, u≤U(t)}because
equation (48), the bootstrap assumptions, and the transport equation (50) imply thatLdΥ = ∂
∂t dΥ
is uniformly bounded inC 1
t,u on the open-at-the-top region{(t, u) 0≤t < T Boot, u≤U(t)}.
Moreover, by (48), we have the following formula, which holds on the closed set{(t, u) 0≤t≤
TBoot, u≤U(t)}:
∂
∂u r=−cµ.(438)
Equations (28a)–(28b) and the bootstrap assumptions (see in particular (334a)) imply that RHS (438)
is bounded from above and below by strictly negative constants. It follows that for each fixed
t∈[0, T Boot], the mapu→r(t, u) is strictly decreasing and thus the map Υ is injective on
{(t, u) 0≤t≤T Boot, u≤U(t)}. In view of (135), we conclude that the extended Υ is aC 2
diffeomorphism on the domain closure{(t, u) 0≤t≤T Boot, u≤U(t)}, as is desired.□
11.10.Proof of the main global existence theorem.We are now ready to prove Theorem 11.3.
Recall Remark 1.5, which implies that it suffices to prove global existence and uniqueness for future-
timest≥0.
By standard local well-posedness and continuation criteria for quasilinear transport equations, if
˚α>0 is sufficiently small, then there exists a maximum timeT Maximal >0 such that (R (+),R (−)) are
classical solutions to (32a)–(32b) for (t, r)∈[0, T Maximal)×[0,∞), such that the eikonal functionu
andµare classical solutions to (316) and (50) respectively for (t, r)∈[0, T Maximal)×[1,∞), and such
that the bootstrap assumptions of Sect. 11.5 hold withT Boot :=T Maximal andε:= ˚α3/4. Since the
estimates of Props. 11.6 and 11.8 hold withT Boot :=T Maximal, ifT Maximal =∞, then also considering
Lemma 11.10, we conclude Theorem 11.3. Hence, we assume for the sake of deriving a contradiction
111

[PDF page 112]
thatT Maximal <∞. Since the estimates of Props. 11.6 and 11.8 hold withT Boot :=T Maximal, none of
the bootstrap assumptions are saturated on the bootstrap domain (t, r)∈[0, T Maximal)×[0,∞); see
Remark 11.9. Moreover, Lemma 11.10 guarantees that the change of variables map Υ(t, u) := (t, r)
extends to aC 2 diffeomorphism onto the closed set (t, r)∈[0, T Maximal]×[1,∞), and the formula
(48) implies that theC 2 norm of Υ is uniformly bounded on the corresponding domain{(t, u) 0≤
t≤T Maximal, u≤U(t)}. In total, we see that theC 2
t,r norm of (R(+),R (−)) is uniformly bounded on
(t, r)∈[0, T Maximal]×[1,∞), and also obeys the uniform (r-weighted-at-the-top-order)C 2
t,r estimates
provided by Prop. 11.6 in the complementary near-time-axis region (t, r)∈[0, T Maximal]×[0,1].
Hence, standard continuation criteria and continuity allow us to extend (R (+),R (−)),u, andµas
classical solutions to a larger slab (t, r)∈[0, T Maximal + ∆)×[0,∞) (for some ∆>0) on which the
bootstrap assumptions hold, thereby contradicting the definition ofT Maximal. We have therefore
shown thatT Maximal =∞and proved the future-global existence results and estimates stated in
Theorem 11.3.
The uniqueness of the solution follows from subtracting two solutions to (32a)–(32b) that share
the same data and applying a standard argument, based on Gr¨ onwall’s inequality and estimates
similar to the ones we used to prove Props. 11.6 and 11.8, to conclude that the difference of the
solutions vanishes for all time; we omit the details.
AppendixA.Extending the results to other profiles
In the gradient-blowup results of Theorem 10.1, we assumed that the Riemann invariant data
were perturbations of− ˚α arctan(r)
r for ˚α>0 sufficiently small, while in the global existence results
of Theorem 11.3, we assumed that the Riemann invariant data were perturbations of ˚α arctan(r)
r ; see
and (63a)–(63b) and (305a)–(305b). What was fundamentally important for the PDE analysis was
not the fine details of the arctan function, but rather only a few key features of it. Hence, in this
appendix, we explain how to extend the results of those two theorems to other “profiles.”
Our starting point is the following definition of an Admissible Profile Seed function; the function
r→arctan(r) that we handled in detail in the bulk of the paper is a special case of such a function.
Definition A.1(Admissible Profile Seed).A functionψ:R→Ris said to be an Admissible
Profile Seed if it satisfies the following properties:
(1) (Regularity of the density at the center of symmetry)ψis odd.
(2) (Sign for positivervalues)ψ(r)>0 whenr >0.
(3) (Global regularity of the profile 37)ψ∈C 3(R).
(4) (Quantitative Monotonicity of the first derivative) There exists aC≥1 such that 1
C(1+r)2 <
ψ′(r)≤ C
(1+r)2 forr∈[0,∞).
(5) (Uniqueness of the crease) On the half-line [0,∞),ψ ′ has a unique, non-degenerate maxi-
mum at a (non-negative) pointr 0.
(6) (Upper bounds for the second and third derivatives). There exists aC >0 such that for
r∈[0,∞), we have|ψ ′′(r)| ≤ C
(1+r)3 and|ψ ′′′(r)| ≤ C
(1+r)4 .
The following properties are simple consequences of Def. A.1:
•(Non-trivial limit at infinity) lim r→∞ ψ(r) exists and is positive and finite. Hence, upon
multiplyingψby a positive constant, we can assume that lim r→∞ ψ(r) = 1. Property 4
above then implies that|ψ(r)−1|≲ 1
1+r, in particular, convergence to the limit at the rate
1
1+r.
37While our proof of Theorem 10.1 assumedC 3 Riemann invariant data, Theorem 11.3 only requires the Riemann
invariant data to beC 2.
112

[PDF page 113]
•(Properties relevant for the structure of the singular boundary near the crease)ψ ′(r0)>0,
ψ′′(r0) = 0, andψ ′′′(r0)<0.
Remark A.2(Other tail rates).It is conceivable that Theorems 10.1 and 11.3 might remain valid
if one makes the following change to Properties 4 and 6: for a fixed constantλ∈(1,2], assume
that there exists aC≥1 such that 1
C(1+r) λ < ψ ′(r)≤ C
(1+r)λ , that|ψ ′′(r)| ≤ C
(1+r)λ+1 , and that
|ψ′′′(r)| ≤ C
(1+r)λ+2 . However, any attempt to prove the theorems with these altered hypotheses
would require substantial modifications to the arguments, and here we do not make any attempt to
rigorously pursue this line of investigation. On the other hand, our proof approach will not work
if we assume thatψ ′(r) decays faster than 1
(1+r)2 asr→ ∞. For example, the remainder term in
(94a) cannot be improved, even if we assume thatψ ′ has a faster decay; this remainder term comes
from integrating the second term on RHS (108) with respect to time. That is, we have:
r˘LR(+) −2µR (+) = 2˚αψ′(u) + O(˚α1+
)
1 +u 2 .(439)
For our proof of Theorem 10.1 to go through, RHS (439) must be non-negative. Thus, we must
haveψ ′(r)≥ 1
C(1+r)2 for someC >0.

We now briefly explain why Theorem 10.1 extends to the case in which the profile (60) is replaced
with the profile on RHS (440), where ˚α>0 is sufficiently small. As before, we can perturb this
profile within the class of data that do not change the decay-in-r-rates, as in (63a)–(63b); we will
ignore these data perturbations because they have no substantial effect on the analysis.
113

[PDF page 114]
The key point is that in geometric (t, u) coordinates, the solution to the nonlinear problem
behaves very similarly to the corresponding solution to the linear system, where the role oft−ris
played by the eikonal functionu; this is essentially what we showed in Prop. 11.6 in the case that
ψ(r) := arctan(r). In particular, because the decay-in-rproperties of the profile fA(r) are the same
as those forA(r) := ˚α arctan(r)
r , for the nonlinear gradient-blowup problem, the decay rates for the
nonlinear solution relative to the geometric coordinates (t, u) will be the same as in Prop. 11.6,
which allows us to control the nonlinear terms perturbatively.
Next, we recall that for the nonlinear problem, the gradient-blowup is driven by the term on
RHS (444a), where the role of the argumentt−ris played by the eikonal functionu. Since ˚α>0
by assumption, theu-value at which the gradient-blowup first occurs in the nonlinear problem is
therefore approximately the value ofuat whichψ ′(u) is as large as possible. In view of Def. A.1,
we see that thisu-value is unique and is approximately equal to−r 0; in Theorem 10.1, we had
ψ(r) := arctan(r) andr 0 = 0; see (137). Moreover, in the general case in which the data are
(440), the singular boundary once again will extend all the way out to spatial infinity because by
assumption,ψ ′(z) is positive for allz >0, and the gradient-blowup along each characteristic is
driven by the positivity of RHS (444a). To obtain the full structure of the singular boundary and
crease, the only other important ingredient is the non-degeneracy of the maximum ofψ ′, which in
the context of Def. A.1 is the property thatψ ′′′(r0)<0. This assumption leads to the positivity of
the analog of the factor 1−3u2
(1+u2)3 on RHS (97c) near the crease, which was crucial for the analysis
near the crease, carried out in Prop. 9.1 (recall that in Theorem 10.1, theu-value of the crease was
approximately 0, and this factor is therefore positive there).
A.2.Extending the results in the global existence case.In this section, we describe how to
extend Theorem 11.3 to a larger set of initial data tied to an Admissible Seed Profile.

We now briefly explain how to extend Theorem 11.3 to the case in which the profile (304) is
replaced with the profile on RHS (445). As before, we can perturb this profile within the class
of data that do not change the decay-in-r-rates, as in (305a)–(305b); we will ignore these data
perturbations because they have no substantial effect on the analysis. The main observation is that
as in Theorem 11.3, since ˚α>0 by assumption, the “dangerous” term−2 ˚αψ′ on RHS (446a) has
a good sign, i.e., in view of Def. A.1, we see that it is everywhere negative. As in Theorem 11.3,
this leads to global-in-space rarefaction, i.e., along each characteristic,µcan grow logarithmically
in time, but not shrink, as in (425a). Hence, when ˚α>0 is sufficiently small, the characteristics
will never intersect, and the solution exists globally and exhibits the same decay rates relative to
the (t, u)-coordinates as in Theorem 11.3.

AppendixB.Global hyperbolicity and MGHDs
In this appendix, we provide an introduction to global hyperbolicity and maximal globally hy-
perbolic developments. The material that is most relevant for the bulk of the paper is Prop. B.14,
in which we prove that the “spherically symmetric” notion of global hyperbolicity of the maximal
development that we proved in Point 7 of Theorem 10.1 is equivalent to the standard notion of
global hyperbolicity from Lorentzian geometry, i.e., we prove Point 8. We also provide a standard
result in Lorentzian geometry, Lemma B.5, which we used in proving Point 9 of Theorem 10.1.
The remaining parts of this appendix provide background material and results that illustrate why
MGHD uniqueness cannot be taken for granted. Spherical symmetry is not important for most of
this appendix, so unless otherwise stated, symmetry is not assumed.
B.1.Lorentzian metrics tied to wave equations and the compressible Euler equations.
To keep the exposition short, we mostly focus the discussion on quasilinear wave systems onR 1+3
of the following form: 38
(g−1)αβ[Φ, ∂Φ]∂α∂βΦ =N[Φ, ∂Φ],(447)
where Φ is the array of unknowns,Nis a smooth nonlinearity, andgis a solution-dependent
Lorentzian metric, that is, a bilinear form of signature (−,+,+,+). Below we explain why the 3D
isentropic and irrotational compressible Euler equations are equivalent to a special case of (447).
It is well-known that wave equations exhibit finite speed of propagation. By this, we mean that
if Ω⊂R 3 is an open subset on which smooth data are given andD(Ω)⊂R 1+3 is a corresponding
domain of dependence (see Def. B.3 for a precise definition) on which a smooth solution exists,
then any other smooth solution that exists on the same setD(Ω) and that has the same data on
Ω must in fact agree with the first solution on all ofD(Ω). In the case of linear or semilinear
wave equations (i.e., when (g−1)αβ is a constant-coefficient Lorentzian metric in (447)),D(Ω) does
not depend on the solution, but rather is completely determined by the set Ω and the “known”
coefficients (g −1)αβ,39 and classical solution non-uniqueness of the type we discuss in Sect. B.4.4
never occurs. However, if the equation isquasilinear(i.e., (g −1)αβ depends on the solution), then
thesolutionand the corresponding dynamic Lorentzian geometry implicitly determine the domain
of dependence. In this appendix, especially in Sect. B.4.4, we will exhibit howD(Ω)’s dependence
on the solution introduces surprising subtleties concerning uniqueness and existence of classical
solutions to (447).
Next, we highlight that in [32, 41], it was shown that the 3Dirrotational and isentropic com-
pressible Euler equations are equivalent to a system of type (447), 40 where the solution-array Φ
comprises the density 41 ϱand the Cartesian velocity componentsv 1, v2, v3, andg=g[Φ] is the
38In (447), we are using Einstein’s summation convention, in which lowercase Greek indices are summed from 0
to 3.
39For example, ifg −1 = diag(−1,1,1,1) is the Minkowski metric, as is the case for the standard linear wave
operator, thenD(Ω) is the set of pointspinR 1+3 such that all straight lines contained in the solid (forward +
backward) light cone with tip atpintersect Ω.
40The compressible Euler equations (15a)–(15b) cannot generally be recast in the form (447), due to the presence
of vorticity. Nevertheless, for all classical solutions, the acoustical metricgdefined in (448) determines the sound
wave characteristics in the system, which are the fastest characteristic; vorticity is transported in the direction of the
material derivative vectorfield (16), which can be shown to always be timelike with respect tog, i.e., vorticity travels
more slowly than sound waves. For these reasons, the notion of global hyperbolicity with respect to the acoustical
metric still applies to all classical solutions to equations (15a)–(15b), even those with non-vanishing vorticity.
41More precisely, in [32, 41], the authors derived a wave equation for the log of the density. However, it is
straightforward (based on the chain rule) to use that equation to derive a wave equation for the density itself.
115

[PDF page 116]
acoustical metric:
g:=−dt⊗dt+c −2
3X
a=1
(dxa −v adt)⊗(dx a −v adt).(448)
The acoustical metric in (448) naturally arises in the study of the characteristics of the compress-
ible Euler equations (15a)–(15b) without symmetry or irrotationality assumptions. More precisely,
straightforward computations (see e.g. [16, Chapter 1] for the details in the case of the relativistic
Euler equations) yield that at each point in spacetime, the characteristic subset of the tangent space
is the following subset of vectors, wheregis the acoustical metric andBis the material derivative
vectorfield from (16):
{Y|g αβY αY β = 0} ∪ {span(B)}.(449)
The subset{Y|g αβY αY β = 0}in (449) is thesound conein the tangent space, i.e., the set of
vectors that are null with respect tog. It is straightforward to check thatBsatisfiesg(B,B) =−1
and hence the subset{span(B)}in (449) lies strictly in the interior of the sound cone, a geometric
representation of the fact that vorticity (which is transported along the integral curves ofB) travels
more slowly than sound waves. For these reasons, even outside the class of spherically symmetric
and irrotational solutions, the intrinsic geometry of compressible Euler flow is governed by the
acoustical metric. Hence, all of the metric-dependent definitions that we introduce below for the
wave system (447) extend to the compressible Euler equations (15a)–(15b) without symmetry or
irrotationality assumptions. However, to keep the exposition short, we will often focus the discussion
only on (447).
For future use, we note that straightforward computations imply that in spherical symmetry, we
have the following identity for the inverse acoustical metric:
g−1 =− 1

whereLandL are the null vectorfields from (29),eS2 is the standard round metric on the Euclidean-

B.2.Basic definitions from Lorentzian geometry.We now provide some basic definitions
needed to rigorously define MGHDs. Throughout this section, we assume thatgis aC 1 Lorentzian
metric defined on an open setM⊂R 1+3.
Definition B.1(Spacelike, timelike, null, and extendibility).
•Vectors:For anyp∈M, we say a tangent spacetime vectorv∈T pMis:
–spacelike (with respect tog) ifg(v, v) :=g αβ(p)vαvβ >0.
–timelike ifg αβ(p)vαvβ <0.
–null ifg αβ(p)vαvβ = 0.
•Hypersurfaces:Let Σ⊂Mbe a hypersurface. Then we say Σ is:
–spacelike if for everyp∈Σ, the normalN p (with respect tog) is a timelike vector.
–timelike if for everyp∈Σ, the normalN p is a spacelike vector.
–null if for everyp∈Σ, the normalN p is a null vector.
•Curves:Letγ:I→Mbe a piecewiseC 1 curve,42 whereI⊆Ris an interval of the form
[a, b],(a, b),[a, b), or (a, b] where−∞ ≤a < b≤ ∞. Then:
42By definition, such curves are continuous onI, and continuously differentiable up to the (possibly empty)
boundary ofIat all but a finite number of points, and for allλ 0 ∈interior(I), lim λ↓λ0 ˙γ(λ) and lim λ↑λ0 ˙γ(λ) exist.
116

[PDF page 117]
–We say thatγis timelike (null) if all its tangent vectors ˙γ(on both sides at points where
˙γexhibits a jump discontinuity) are timelike (null). We sayγis causal if all its tangent
vectors (including on both sides at points where ˙γexhibits a jump discontinuity) are
timelike or null.
–We say thatγisextendibleinMif at least one of the limits lim s→a γ(s) or lim s→b γ(s)
existinM. We stress that this notion of extendibility depends on thedomainM.
–Ifγis not extendible, we say that it is inextendible.
For the next definition, we note that the set of timelike vectors atp, i.e.,T(p) :={v∈
TpM|g(v, v)<0}, forms a disconnected double-cone.
Definition B.2(Time orientation, future, and past).Atime orientationof the Lorentzian manifold
(M,g) is a continuous choice of a positive componentT(p) for eachp∈M. We call causal vectors
that lie in the closure of the positive componentfuture-directed, and those that lie in the closure of
the negative componentpast-directed. For eachp∈M, we define the timelike (causal)futureI +(p)
(J +(p)) to be the set of points which can be reached frompby a future-directed timelike (causal)
curve, i.e., a piecewiseC 1, timelike (causal) curve whose tangent vectors are always future-directed.
The timelike (causal)pastI −(p) (J −(p)) is defined analogously.
Definition B.3(Domain of dependence).LetM⊂R 1+3 be an open set, letgbe aC 1 Lorentzian
metric onM, and let Ω⊂M. We defineD(Ω), thedomain of dependenceof Ω (inM) as follows:
D(Ω) :={q∈M|every inextendible, piecewiseC 1, causal curve throughqintersects Ω}.(451)
With the “causality notions” from above in hand, we can now define global hyperbolicity and
Cauchy hypersurfaces.
Definition B.4(Cauchy hypersurfaces and Globally hyperbolic regions).LetM⊂R 1+3 be an
open set, and letgbe aC 1 Lorentzian metric onM.
•We say that a hypersurface Σ⊂Mis aCauchy hypersurface(with respect tog) forMif
every timelike, piecewiseC 1, time-oriented,43 inextendible curve inMintersects Σ exactly
once.
•We say that (M,g) isglobally hyperbolicwheneverMcontains a Cauchy hypersurface.
The following is a basic result from Lorentzian geometry; see [36, Chapter 14, Lemma 29] for a
proof.
Lemma B.5(D(Σ) =Mfor Cauchy hypersurfaces).LetM⊂R 1+3 be an open set, and letg
be aC 1 Lorentzian metric onM. Assume thatΣ⊂Mis a Cauchy hypersurface in the sense of
Def. B.4. ThenD(Σ) =M.
Remark B.6(Inextendible causal curves intersect Cauchy hypersurfaces).In our proof of Theo-
rem 10.1, we needed Lemma B.5 only because of the following simple implication, which follows
from Def. B.3 and the lemma: if Σ is a Cauchy hypersurface, then any inextendible causal curve in
Mmust intersect Σ.
Definition B.7(Globally hyperbolic developments of data on spacelike hypersurfaces).For equa-
tion44 (447), a globally hyperbolic development (GHD) of initial data ( ˚Φ1, ˚Φ2) posed on a spacelike
43A time-oriented piecewiseC 1 curveγ:I→Mis one where, for allλ∈interior(I), the vectors lim λ↓λ0 ˙γ(λ) and
limλ↑λ0 ˙γ(λ) all lie in the same connected component ofI(γ(λ 0)).
44A GHD for the compressible Euler equations (15a)–(15b) is defined in an analogous fashion, where the metric
is the acoustical metric (448) and, since the system is first-order, the data are (ϱ, v 1, v2, v3)↾ Σ. The remaining
definitions that we provide in this appendix for equation (447) also extend to equations (15a)–(15b) in an obvious
fashion.
117

[PDF page 118]
hypersurface Σ is a pair (M,Φ), whereMis an open neighborhood of Σ, Φ is a classical solu-
tion45 to (447) defined onMsuch that (M,g[Φ, ∂Φ]) is globally hyperbolic with Σ as a Cauchy
hypersurface, and (Φ↾ Σ,NΦ↾ Σ) = (˚Φ1, ˚Φ2), whereNis theg-unit normal to Σ.
The following theorem shows that GHDs always exist for smooth data on spacelike Cauchy
hypersurfaces. The proof relies on standard local well-posedness for the Cauchy problem for the
wave equation (447).
Theorem B.8(GHDs exist for data on Cauchy hypersurfaces, [19, Theorem 4.75]).Given smooth
initial data( ˚Φ1, ˚Φ2)for the wave equation(447)on a spacelike hypersurfaceΣ⊂R 1+3 as in
Def. B.7, there exists a GHD(M,Φ).
Remark B.9(Globally hyperbolic developments for data on other families of hypersurfaces).In
Def. B.7 for the sake of brevity, we chose to provide the definition of GHD only forspacelikeCauchy
hypersurfaces, since our main results concern GHDs of data given on the spacelike hypersurface
{t= 0}. However, this definition could be extended to other kinds of causal Cauchy hypersurfaces.
For example, a common physically relevant choice is a spacelike-characteristic Cauchy hypersurface,
i.e., a piecewise-smooth surface consisting of a spacelike portion and a null portion that intersect
transversally along a co-dimension 2 submanifold. This is the type of Cauchy hypersurface that
we studied in the regionIin the proof of Theorem 10.1. Another well-known choice of a Cauchy
hypersurface consists of a pair of two transversal null hypersurfaces that intersect transversally in
a co-dimension 2 topological sphere. In the latter case, the initial value problem for (447) is called
thecharacteristic initial value problem.
We point out that for non-spacelike Cauchy hypersurfaces, the initial data typically have to be
posed in a different way compared to the spacelike case. 46 Although a full treatment of these
issues is beyond the scope of this article, we note that data posed on spacelike-characteristic
and characteristic-characteristic Cauchy hypersurfaces typically must satisfy constraint equations
as well as compatibility conditions on the co-dimension 2 submanifold on which they intersect
transversally.47 These compatibility requirements would have to be included in the correspond-
ing GHD definitions. However, there is a specific case in which these additional constraints and
compatibility conditions are automatically satisfied:whenever it is already known that the solution
exists in an open neighborhood of the Cauchy hypersurface in question. In this case, the constraint
equations and compatibility conditions can be immediately inferred by simply tracing the known
solution on the Cauchy hypersurface. We have highlighted these issues because they appeared (in
a very mild form in the context of spherical symmetry) in our proof of Theorem 10.1.
The following definition, taken from [19, Section 4.7], captures a form of uniqueness for a GHD.
Definition B.10(Unique GHDs).Given initial data ( ˚Φ1, ˚Φ2) for equation (447), we call a GHD
(M,Φ) a unique globally hyperbolic development (UGHD) iff for all other GHDs ( fM,eΦ) of the
same data, we have we have Φ = eΦ onM∩ fM.
Intuitively, failure of MGHD uniqueness might happen whenMis too “big.” However, as we
will explain in Sect. B.4.5, in principle, there might be smooth data such that not even a single
UGHD, however “small,” exists!
45Here, classical means “C 2.” One can define GHDs for solutions other than classical ones, e.g., for solutions
belonging to function spaces in which local well-posedness holds.
46For example, the normal vectors which are null cannot be made to be of unit length, so (Φ↾Σ,NΦ↾ Σ) = (˚Φ1, ˚Φ2)
does not make sense for characteristic initial data.
47For example, letC 0 :={t=x 1},C 0 :={t=−x 1}, and considerf∈C 2(C0) andg∈C 2(C0). Then it is easy
to check that the linear wave equation−∂ 2

Definition B.11(GHD Extendibility).For equation (447), a GHD (M,Φ) of initial data ( ˚Φ1, ˚Φ2)
posed on a spacelike hypersurface Σ is said to beextendibleif there exists another GHD ( fM,eΦ) of
the (same) data on Σ such that the following hold:
•M⊊ fM
• eΦ↾ M= Φ
Definition B.12(Maximal globally hyperbolic development).For equation (447), a GHD (MMax,Φ Max)
of data on Σ is said to be amaximal globally hyperbolic development(MGHD) if it fails to
be extendible in the sense of Def. B.11.
The following theorem shows that MGHDs always exist for smooth data on spacelike Cauchy
hypersurfaces. The proof is based on applying Zorn’s lemma to chains of GHDs.
Theorem B.13(At least one MGHD exists for data on Cauchy hypersurfaces).If(M,Φ)is a GHD
of smooth initial data on a Cauchy hypersurfaceΣas in Def. B.7 (and as afforded by Theorem B.8),
then there exists at least one MGHD(M Max,Φ Max)in the sense of Def. B.12 that is an extension
of(M,Φ)in the sense of Def. B.11.
Proof.LetGdenote the set of globally hyperbolic developments of the initial data on Σ that are
extensions in the sense of Def. B.11 of the fixed GHD (M,Φ).Gis non-empty since (M,Φ) is an
element. Partially order the elements ofGby extension, i.e., the partial ordering is defined by
(M1,Φ 1)≤(M 2,Φ 2)⇐ ⇒M 1 ⊂M 2 and Φ2 ↾M1= Φ1. LetCbe a chain, i.e., a totally ordered
subset ofG. LetM Upper denote the union of all the solution manifolds of the elements ofC, and
define the function ΦUpper by ΦUpper(p) =eΦ(p) wheneverp∈ fMand (fM,eΦ)∈C. SinceCis totally
ordered by assumption, it is straightforward to see that Φ Upper is well-defined.
We will now prove that (MUpper,g[Φ Upper, ∂ΦUpper]) is a globally hyperbolic manifold containing Σ
as a Cauchy hypersurface. To this end, we letγ:I→M Upper be an inextendibleg[Φ Upper, ∂ΦUpper]-
timelike, time-oriented, piecewiseC 1 curve (whereI= (a, b) is an open interval with−∞ ≤a < b≤
∞) inM Upper that containspas an image point. Then, there exists an fMsuch that (fM,eΦ)∈Cand
p∈ fM. Let eI⊂Ibe the largest sub-interval ofIsuch thatp∈γ( eI)⊂ fM, and set eγ:=γ↾ eI. Note
thateImust be an open interval of the form (ea,eb)⊂(a, b) because fMis open andγis continuous. We
claim thateγis inextendible in fM. Suppose not. Then one of the limits lim s→eaeγ(a) or lim s→ebeγ(s)
must exist in fM, and therefore also inM Upper. Without loss of generality, we assume that the
former limit exists. Since fMis open, there exists an open ballUcontaining eγ(ea) such thatU⊂ fM.
By the continuity and inextendibility ofγ, for curve parameter valuessin an open neighborhood
ofea,γ(s) is defined and satisfiesγ(s)∈U. That is, there is an interval eI ′ such that eI⊊ eI ′ ⊂I
and such thatγ: eI ′ →fM. Since eIis a strict subset of eI ′, this contradicts the maximality of eI. We
have therefore shown by contradiction thateγis inextendible in fM. Moreover, since Φ Upper ↾fM=eΦ,
it follows that eγisg[ eΦ, ∂eΦ]-timelike. Thus, since ( fM,eΦ) is a GHD of the data on Σ, eγmust
intersect Σ exactly once. Clearly this implies that the original curveγintersects Σ. We will now
show thatγcannot intersect Σ two or more times. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there
exist (finite) numbersA < Bsuch that [A, B]⊂Iand such thatγ(A),γ(B)∈Σ.γ([A, B]) is
compact subset ofM Upper and therefore must be contained in some fMwith ( fM,eΦ)∈C. This is
impossible sinceγ↾ [A,B] is time-oriented andg[ eΦ, ∂eΦ]-timelike infM, and therefore can intersect Σ
at most once. In total, we have shown that Σ is a Cauchy hypersurface forM Upper (with respect
tog[Φ Upper, ∂ΦUpper]) in the sense of Definition B.4. We have shown that (M Upper,Φ Upper) is a
GHD of the data that extends (M,Φ), which by definition means that (M Upper,Φ Upper)∈G. By
construction, it follows that (M Upper,Φ Upper) is an upper bound forC, that is, for any ( fM,eΦ)∈C,
119

[PDF page 120]
we have (fM,eΦ)≤(M Upper,Φ Upper). In particular, every chainC⊂Ghas an upper bound inG.
Hence, Zorn’s lemma yields a maximal element (MMax,Φ Max)∈G, i.e., a GHD of the data on Σ such
that any other (cM,bΦ)∈Gthat is comparable to (M Max,Φ Max) satisfies (cM,bΦ)≤(M Max,Φ Max).
It follows that (M Max,Φ Max) is a MGHD in the sense of Def. B.12.

B.3.M Max is globally hyperbolic in the sense of Sect. B.2.In Point 7 of Theorem 10.1, in
spherical symmetry, we gave a definition of global hyperbolicity in terms of integral curves ofL
andL and proved thatM Max is globally hyperbolic in that sense. We deferred the proof of Point 8
of Theorem 10.1, which is an equivalent notion of global hyperbolicity, until the present section.
In the next proposition, we prove Point 8, which was needed for the proof of the final point of
Theorem 10.1.
Proposition B.14(Equivalent notions of global hyperbolicity).LetM Max be the maximal de-
velopment region from Theorem 10.1, viewed as a subset of(t, r)-coordinate space, and letS:
R×[0,∞)×S 2 →R 1+3 be the map from spherical coordinates to Cartesian coordinates, i.e.,
S(t, r, ω) = (t, rω).(452)
ThenS(M Max ×S 2)⊂R 1+3 is globally hyperbolic with respect to the acoustical metricg(see(448))
in the sense of Def. B.4, whereS(Σ 0 ×S 2) ={t= 0} ⊂R 1+3 is a Cauchy hypersurface in the sense
of Def. B.4.
Proof.
Step 1: Setup for an arbitrary inextendible, timelike, piecewiseC 1 curveγ . Letγbe any time-
oriented, inextendible, piecewiseC 1,g-timelike curve inS(M Max×S2)⊂R 1+3. Note thatS(M Max×
S2) is a spherically symmetric open subset ofR 1+3, but the curveγcan have non-trivial angular
dependence. It is straightforward to check that the material derivative vectorfieldBdefined in
(16) isg-normal to constant-time surfaces and satisfiesg(B,B) =−1 andBt= 1. That is,B
isg-timelike, andtis a time function (its gradient vectorfield is everywhere non-vanishing and
g-timelike). Hence, sinceγis ag-timelike curve by assumption, without loss of generality, we can
assume thatγis parameterized byt, i.e., the curve ist→γ(t), i.e., the time componentγ 0 ofγ
satisfiesγ 0(t) =t. LetIdenote the maximal interval of time on whichγis defined and remains in
S(MMax ×S 2). In particular,γ:I→S(M Max ×S 2). Without loss of generality, we can assume
that a timet 1 >0 belongs to the domainIofγ, and we setq:=γ(t 1) andI + :=I∩[0, t 1]; the case
in which there is at 1 <0 in the domainIcan be handled with similar arguments. Moreover,γis
continuous onIand, away from a finite number of time valuest ∗ ∈I, is differentiable with timelike
tangent vector ˙γ. Finally, for sucht ∗ belonging to the interior ofI, the “upper” tangent vector
limt↓t∗ ˙γ(t) and “lower” tangent vector lim t↑t∗ ˙γ(t) are both assumed to exist and beg-timelike.
While the upper and lower tangent vectors might disagree, this has no effect on the subsequent
analysis. We will therefore abuse notation and denote either tangent vector by ˙γ; in practice, this
will not cause any confusion.
Step 2: Continuous extension ofγto a closed domain . To prove the proposition, we must show
that astdecreases fromt 1,γ(t) intersects{t= 0}before escapingS

B.4.Subtleties of existence, uniqueness, and non-uniqueness for GHDs and MGHDs
of the data.There is currently no established general theorem for the proving the uniqueness
of MGHDs for solutions to (447) or (15a)–(15b). This is in stark contrast to Einstein’s equations
of mathematical General Relativity, where in the historic works [13, 14] by Choquet-Bruhat and
Choquet-Bruhat–Geroch, the Einstein-vacuum equations48 were formulated as an initial value prob-
lem starting from data on a 3Dmanifold Σ, and existence and uniqueness of an MGHD was proved.
Initial data for Einstein’s equations is a triple (Σ, g, k), where Σ is a connected 3-dimensional man-
ifold,gis a Riemannian metric on Σ, andkis a symmetric type
 0
2

-tensor on Σ satisfying certain
constraints.49 The PDE unknowns for the Einstein-vacuum equations are the following two objects:
(I) The Lorentzian manifoldM, on which the solution is defined 50, together with an embedding
ι: Σ⊂M.
(II) The components of a Lorentzian metricgonMthat satisfies the Einstein-vacuum equations:
Ric(g) = 0,(454)
where Ric(g) is the Ricci curvature ofg. Moreover, withkdenoting the second fundamental
form ofι(Σ) with respect tog, and withι ∗ denoting pullback byι, we must haveι ∗g=g
andι ∗k=k, i.e., the solution must “take on the given initial data.”
We now highlight that Point (I) is very different compared to equation (447), which is a PDE
posed on thefixed backgroundspacetimeR 1+3. That is, for Einstein’s equations,there is no given
background or “ambient” spacetime; the spacetime manifold must be constructed evolutionarily
alongside the gravitational metricgwhich satisfies (454). It is precisely this distinction that
prevents one from directly extending Choquet-Bruhat–Geroch’s MGHD existence and uniqueness
result for the Einstein-vacuum equations to equation (447). For similar reasons, their result is not
guaranteed to extend to the compressible Euler equations (15a)–(15b). The rest of this appendix
is dedicated to further explaining these issues and to showing that it is impossible to prove a
MGHD uniqueness result for general wave equations of type (447). As we will explain, the basic
obstacle turns out to be that for (447), two distinct GHDs of the same data can have adisconnected
intersection; in Sect. B.4.3, we explain why this difficulty never arises in an MGHD construction
for Einstein’s equations. Much of our discussion here is based on [19,37]. 51
B.4.1.Local vs. global uniqueness for GHDs for(447).The standard local existence and uniqueness
theorems for (447), which are based on energy estimates, imply the following “local” uniqueness
result.
Proposition B.15(Local uniqueness, [19, Prop. 4.63]).Given two GHDs(M 1,Φ 1)and(M 2,Φ 2)
of the same initial data of(447), there is acommon globally hyperbolic development(CGHD)
(V,Φ)of the same data such thatV⊂M 1 ∩M 2 withΦ = Φ 1|V = Φ2|V.
Note that Prop. B.15 yields a ratherweakform of uniqueness, for it allows, in principle, the
existence of a third GHD (M 3,Φ 3) of the same data such that there exists ap∈M 3 ∩Vwith
Φ3(p)̸= Φ 1(p) = Φ2(p). This is clearly unsatisfying from the point of view of classical determinism.
48Choquet-Bruhat–Geroch’s results in fact hold for many matter models.
49The tensorsgandkmust satisfy certain constraint PDEs obtained from the Gauss–Codazzi equations, Einstein’s
equations (454), and the assumptions thatι ∗g=gandι ∗k=k.
50A posteriori, the MGHD manifold (in fact, any globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold) can be shown to be
homeomorphic toI×Σ, whereI⊂Ris an interval.
51Sbierski’s paper [37] proved the results of [13], but without using Zorn’s lemma.
123

[PDF page 124]
The following theorem is one of the main results of [19], which, under a very strong assumption
that turns out to not always be true, 52 upgrades Prop. B.15 to “global” uniqueness. As we explain
in Sect. B.4.2, if one could find a way to guarantee that the assumption always holds, then this
notion of global uniqueness would be sufficient forboththe existence and uniqueness of the MGHD
for (447).
Theorem B.16(Global uniqueness under a connectedness assumption, [19, Theorem 4.64]).Let
(M1,Φ 1)and(M 2,Φ 2)be two GHDs of the same initial data of(447). IfM 1 ∩M 2 isconnected,
thenΦ 1 = Φ2 onM 1 ∩M 2.
Sketch of Proof.Complete details are given in [19, Theorem 4.64]. Here, we just sketch the proof
to illustrate the main ideas.
Step 1: Consider the set{(V α,Φ α)|α∈A}of all CGHDs of the same data, whereAis a non-empty
index set by Prop. B.15. LetV ∞ :=S
α∈A Vα and Φ ∞(p) = Φ α(p) forp∈V α. It is clear
that Φ ∞ is well defined and that (V ∞,Φ ∞) is a CGHD. Moreover, it is clear thatV ∞ is
globally hyperbolic. The goal is to prove thatV ∞ =M 1 ∩M 2.
Step 2: Suppose not and thatV ∞ ⊊M 1 ∩M2. Then there exists a boundary pointp∈∂V ∞ ∩M1 ∩
M2.It is precisely the existence of thispwhere the assumption thatM 1 ∩M2 is connected is
used. It can then be shown (see [19, Theorem 4.64]) thatpis a “spacelike” point in the sense
that there exists a spacelike hypersurfaceΣcontainingpsuch thatΣ⊂ V∞ ∩M 1 ∩M 2.
Since Φ1 and Φ2 and their derivatives agree onΣ, we can uniquely solve the equations (via
a standard method based on energy estimates and iterations) in a small neighborhood of
Σ, obtaining a larger CGHD, thereby contradicting the maximality ofV ∞.
□
B.4.2.MGHD uniqueness for(447)if the hypotheses of Theorem B.16 are known to always hold.
To illustrate a point, let us assume that the connectedness hypothesis of Theorem B.16 holds for
all smooth data and all MGHD pairs (M 1,Φ 1) and (M 2,Φ 2). This is a very strong assumption,
not easy to verify or falsify, that fails for some equations; see Sect. B.4.4. In any case, under this
assumption, we will explain why it is fairly easy to both construct and prove the existence and
uniqueness of the MGHD for data,M Max :=S
ι∈A{Mι |ι∈A}, where the union is taken over all the
spacetime regions of all GHDs (M ι,Φ ι) of the data. Here,ι∈Ais a non-empty index set because
at least one GHD always exists; see Theorem B.8. Under our very strong assumption, we can use
Theorem B.16 to construct a well-defined function ΦMax onM Max, as was highlighted in [19, Remark
4.78]. Indeed, given initial data to (447), one can define Φ Max by ΦMax(p) := Φι(p) forι∈A, which
solves (447) because each Φι does. The proof of [19, Theorem 4.77] then implies that (M Max,Φ Max)
is indeed a globally hyperbolic development of the data. By construction, (M Max,Φ Max) is maximal
and unique.
The following theorem is the main result from [19] that we use in this paper to prove uniqueness
of the MGHDM Max in Theorem 10.1. Roughly, an MGHD is unique provided that it lies on one
side of its boundary.
Theorem B.17(Uniqueness condition for MGHDs, [19, Theorem 4.82]).Let(M Max,Φ Max)be an
MGHD of initial data posed on a connected spacelike hypersurfaceΣfor(447)(such an MGHD
exists by Theorem B.13). Assume that for everyp∈∂M Max, there exists a neighborhoodU⊂R 1+3
ofp, a coordinate chartf:U→(−ϵ, ϵ) 3,ϵ >0, and a continuous functiong: (−ϵ, ϵ) 3 →(−ϵ, ϵ)
such that the following “one-sidedness” assumptions hold:
•∂M Max ∩U=f −1(graphg).
52The paper [19] identified a sub-class of equations of type (447) for which the assumption always is true. The
sub-class was called “super-luminal.”
124

[PDF page 125]
•All pointsbelowthe graph ofgare mapped intoM Max.
•All pointsabovethe graph ofgare mapped toR 1+3 \M Max.
ThenM Max is theuniqueMGHD of the data. That is, any other GHD( fM,eΦ)satisfies fM⊂M Max
andΦ Max ↾fM=eΦ.
Discussion of the ideas in the proof.While we will not provide the proof of [19, Theorem 4.82] here,
we will provide some insight into the role that the “one-sidedness” assumptions play in the proof.
We will argue by contradiction. That is, we suppose that (M Max

B.4.3.Einstein’s equations.In this section, we give an overview of why for Einstein’s equations, a
global uniqueness result – similar to the one from Theorem B.16 for equation (447) but distinct in
ways that we will explain – always holds; see [37] for the rigorous details. We will denote solutions
to Einstein’s equations by (M,g) to emphasize that we are looking for GHDs of initial data that
satisfy points (I)–(II) from Sect. B.4. In particular,Mitself is part of the solution. The main
subtlety is that, given two solutions (M 1,g 1) and (M 2,g 2), the intersectionM 1 ∩M 2 is a priori
not even defined. This prevents one from being able to directly apply Theorem B.16 to Einstein’s
equations.
Despite this difficulty, theredoesexist a satisfying way of formulating global uniqueness for
Einstein’s equations that allows for analog of Theorem B.16 to be provedwithout the restrictive
hypothesis on the connectedness ofM 1 ∩M 2. We state this below as Theorem B.21.
Before we can discuss Theorem B.21 for Einstein’s equations, we must first discuss what it means
to “extend” GHDs for Einstein’s equations. The main idea is to consider isometric extensions of
solutions. The key point is that unlike equations of type (447), Einstein’s equations are invariant
under the diffeomorphism group. That is, if (M,g) is a solution to the Cauchy problem (I)–(II)
from Sect. B.4 andψ:M→Nis any smooth diffeomorphism, then (N, ψ ∗g) is also a solution,
whereψ ∗g= (ψ −1)∗gdenotes the pushforward ofgbyψ. In this way, one can view solving
Einstein’s equations as finding an isometry class of Lorentzian manifolds satisfying (I)–(II).
Definition B.18(Extensions of GHDs of data for Einstein’s equations).Given two GHDs (M,g)
and (M ′,g ′) of the same initial data for Einstein’s equations, we say (M ′,g ′) is anextension
53MMax
1 ∪M Max
2 is connected as long as Σ is, for thenM Max
1 andM Max
1 are connected; see the discussion in the
Step 3 of the proof discussion of Theorem B.21.
125

[PDF page 126]
of (M,g) if there exists a time-orientation-preserving isometric embedding 54 ψ:M→M ′ that
preserves the initial data.
In her celebrated paper [14], Choquet-Bruhat showed that given initial data for Einstein’s equa-
tions, there exists a GHD (M,g). The same paper also yields a local uniqueness result for Einstein’s
equations, i.e., an analog of Prop. B.15, which we present as the following proposition in the lan-
guage of “common globally hyperbolic developments” (CGHDs). Informally, given a pair of GHDs
of the same data, a CGHD is a third GHD that is “contained” in both of them.
Proposition B.19(Local uniqueness for Einstein’s equations).Given two GHDs(M 1,g 1),(M 2,g 2)
of the same data, there exists acommon globally hyperbolic development(CGHD)(M,g), that
is,55 M⊂M 1, and(M 1,g 1)and(M 2,g 2)arebothextensions of(M,g)in the sense of Def. B.18.
Remark B.20(Uniqueness up to isometry).Because of the role played by isometric embeddings
in the conclusion of Prop. B.19, the proposition can be thought of as “uniqueness up to isometry.”
We now provide Theorem B.21, which is the main “global uniqueness” result for Einstein’s
equations. The theorem is proved in detail in [37, Theorem 2.7]], whereas here, we only present
some of the main ideas; we are mainly interested in the differences between Einstein’s equations
and equation (447). We stress again that the fundamental difference between Theorems B.21 and
B.16 is that the connectedness ofM 1 ∩M 2 from Theorem B.16does not have a natural analogin
the context of Einstein’s equations.
Theorem B.21(Global uniqueness for Einstein’s equations, [37, Theorem 2.7]).Given two GHDs
(M1,g 1)and(M 2,g 2)of the same initial data for Einstein’s equations, there exists a GHD(M,g)
which is an extension, in the sense of Def. B.18, of both(M 1,g 1)and(M 2,g 2).
Sketch of the proof.
Step 1: Consider the set{(M α,g α)|α∈A}of all CGHDs of (M 1,g 1) and (M 2,g 2), whereAis a
non-empty index set by Prop. B.19. Letψ α :M α →M 1 be the canonical embedding (the
identity map) of eachM α intoM 1. It is not difficult to see thatV ∞ :=S
α∈A Mα, equipped
with the metricgdefined byg:=g 1 ↾Mα=g α, is a well-defined CGHD of (M 1,g 1) and
(M2,g 2); see [37, Theorem 3.4] for a detailed proof. In addition, by construction, it is
maximal and unique in the following sense: (V ∞,g) is an extension of any other CGHD of
(M1,g 1) and (M2,g 2). The reader should compare this portion of the proof with Step 1 of
Theorem B.16. The main new step needed here is to first embed theM α into one of the
GHDs in question (indeed, this is “built into” the definition of a CGHD from Prop. B.19),
so that the union is a well-defined GHD of the data.
Step 2: The most technical part of the paper [37] is proving the analog of Step 2 from our proof
sketch of Theorem B.16. We now describe the main ideas. Let (V,g) be a CGHD of
two GHDs (M 1,g 1) and (M 2,g 2), and letψ:V→M 2 be the isometric embedding
corresponding to the extension intoM2. Recall thatV⊂M 1 by assumption; see Prop. B.19.
We say that boundary pointsp 1 ∈∂Vandp 2 ∈∂ψ(V) arecorresponding boundary points
if and only if given any open neighborhoodV 1 ofp 1 andV 2 ofp 2,V 1 ∩ψ −1(V2 ∩ψ(V))̸=
∅.56 Then, similar (but much more technically challenging) methods as in Step 2 of our
54Isometric embeddings are such that the pullback ofψ ∗g′ =g, whereψ ∗ denotes pullback byψ.
55This definition of a CGHD breaks the diffeomorphism invariance because we are requiring thatMis a realized
as a subset ofM 1. However, because solutions are unique only up to diffeomorphism invariance anyway, this does
not cause any problems in the theory; see [37, Remark 2.5] for a detailed discussion.
56We clarify that this technical definition is was not needed in Step 2 of Theorem B.16 because there,p∈
∂V∞ ∩M 1 ∩M 2 was a boundary point in a well-defined intersection (since there,M 1 andM 2 were both subsets of
the same ambient spaceR 1+3).
126

[PDF page 127]
proof sketch of Theorem B.16 can be used to prove the following: if a CGHD (V,g) has
corresponding boundary pointsp 1, p2 in bothM 1 andM 2, then there exists a strictly
larger extension of (V,g) that is also a CGHD of (M 1,g 1) and (M2,g 2); see [37, Theorem
3.6].57 Equivalently, if a CGHD of (M 1,g 1) and (M 2,g 2) is maximal, then there are no
corresponding boundary points.
We note that this Step 2, though similar in spirit to Step 2 of Theorem B.16, is distinct
precisely becausewe are not trying to prove thatV ∞ =M 1 ∩M2; as we have stressed,M 1 ∩
M2 is not even a priori well-defined. Instead, we are constructing CGHDs via Prop. B.19,
using the definitions of GHD extensions given in Def. B.18.
Step 3: Roughly speaking, one finishes the proof of Theorem B.21 by constructing a common ex-
tension of bothM 1 andM 2, obtained by “gluing” them together along their maximal
CGHD manifoldV ∞. We now provide an overview on how this is achieved in [37]. Let
ψ1 :V ∞ →M 1 andψ 2 :V ∞ →M 2 be the isometric embeddings corresponding to the fact
that (M 1,g 1) and (M 2,g 2) are both extensions of (V ∞,g). Consider the disjoint union
M1 ⊔M 2 endowed with the natural topology. Then forp, q∈M 1 ⊔M 2, we definep∼q
if and only ifp=ψ 1(ψ−1
2 (q)), orq=ψ 2(ψ−1
1 (p)), orp=q. [37, Theorem 2.7] proves that
the quotient manifoldM:= (M 1 ⊔M 2)/∼is the desired GHD which extends bothM 1 and
M2. In particular, the most subtle step is to prove thatMis Hausdorff, which Sbierski
proves using Step 2; see [37, Sect. 3.3]. The connectedness ofMfollows easily from that of
the connectedness of the Cauchy hypersurface Σ; for it is easy to prove thatMis globally
hyperbolic, and all globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifolds with a Cauchy hypersurface Σ
are diffeomorphic toR×Σ [9] and hence are path-connected whenever Σ is.
□
We are now ready to state the main theorem from [37].
Theorem B.22(Existence and uniqueness of an MGHD for Einstein’s equations; [37, Theorem
2.8]).Given initial data for Einstein’s equations, there exists a GHD(M Max,g Max)that is an
extension, in the sense of Def. B.18, of any other GHD of the same data. This GHD is unique up
to isometry, and is called the MGHD of the data.
Discussion of the ideas in the proof.At a broad level, to prove Theorem B.22, one adapts the ideas
in the paragraph at the start of Sect. B.4.2. The key difference is that the “maximal element” is
not obtained by unions, but rather by gluing all GHDs together along their maximal CGHD. There
is one important caveat in this procedure, explained in [37, Sect. 3.3]: the collection of all GHDs
is not a set, but rather forms aproper class. This issue did not arise in Step 2 of our proof sketch
of Theorem B.21 because there, the union was taken over the collection of GHDs that embed into
thefixedmanifoldsM 1 andM 2, and this collectionisa set. In proving [37, Theorem 2.8], Sbierski
doesnotconsider the collection of all GHDs of the initial data on the Cauchy hypersurface Σ, but
rather, GHDs whose spacetime manifolds are subsets of Σ×R. In [37, Sect. 3.3], it is shown that
this collectionisa set, denoted here byX, and one can therefore “glue” all the maximal CGHDs
as in the proof of Theorem B.21, thereby constructing a maximal element (M Max,g Max)∈X. The
proof of maximality and uniqueness – up to isometry – concludes by proving that any GHD of the
same initial data isometrically embeds into the specific one (M Max,g Max).□
We close this section by highlighting two important difficulties that would arise if one attempted
to modify the proof of Theorem B.21 to apply to the wave system (447) in the case thatM 1 ∩M 2
is disconnected in the ambient spacetimeR 1+3. First, one can show [19] that given two GHDs
57To construct this larger extension, one exploits the existence of corresponding boundary pointsp 1, p2 to prove
thatp 1 ∈∂V∩ψ −1(p2) is a “spacelike” point in the sense that there is a spacelike hypersurface Σ⊆ Vwithp 1 ∈Σ.
By locally solving Einstein’s equations on Σ, we obtain a strictly larger CGHD.
127

[PDF page 128]
for equation (447) as in the hypotheses of Prop. B.15, the maximal CGHD ofM 1 ∩M 2 is only
the connected component that contains the Cauchy hypersurface. Second, we expect that if one
tried to glueM 1 andM 2 along their maximal CGHD, this would yield a solution definednoton a
subset ofR 1+3, but rather on a manifold which projects down onR 1+3 and contains the connected
components ofM 1 ∩M 2 twice.
It is an interesting open problem to rigorously construct and make sense of such
“multi-valued” solutions to (447).
B.4.4.The E–R–S example of MGHD non-uniqueness for a quasilinear wave equation.In this
final section, we present a fascinating result from [19, Theorem 3.45], discussed already in the
introduction, which provides a constructive example of a quasilinear wave (447) and two GHDs
(M1,Φ 1),(M2,Φ 2) of the same smooth data such thatM 1 ∩M 2 is disconnected and such that Φ 1
disagreeswith Φ 2 on part ofM 1 ∩M 2.
Theorem B.23([19, Theorem 3.45]).Consider the following quasilinear wave equation onR 1+1:
(h−1)αβ[∂Φ]∂α∂βΦ = 0,(455)
where relative to the standard Cartesian coordinates(x 0, x1) := (t, x1),(h −1)αβ are the components
of the following inverse Lorentzian metric:

−(1 + (∂1Φ)2)∂ tΦ∂1Φ

.(456)
Then there existsC ∞ Cauchy initial data(Φ| t=0, ∂tΦ|t=0) = ( ˚Φ1, ˚Φ2)for(455)and two GHDs
(M1,Φ 1),(M2,Φ 2)such thatM 1 ∩M 2 is disconnected and such that there exists ap∈M 1 ∩M 2
withΦ 1(p)̸= Φ 2(p); see Fig. 9.
Equation (455) is known as theBorn–Infeld equation(BIE). It appears as a model in various
contexts, including nonlinear electrodynamics and cosmological string theory. It is well known that
the wave equation (455) satisfies a very strong “doublenull condition,” i.e., there are no resonant
interaction terms in the equations, and in particular, the non-uniqueness from Theorem B.23 is not
tied to the formation of a shock. We do point out, however, that in the example of Theorem B.23,
each of the two solutions forms a singularity that is more severe than a shock, corresponding to the
blowup of the Lorentzian metric itself, e.g., the gradients∂Φ 1 and∂Φ 2 both blow up at the black
dot in Fig. 9; see [19, Section 3.6] for further details. 58
We also highlight thatM 1 andM 2 failthe “one-sidedness” hypothesis of Theorem B.17 for
guaranteeing uniqueness because they both lie on “both sides” of the solid red and blue curves in
Fig. 9. To provide further insight into how this “two-sidedness” is tied to the lack-of-uniqueness, we
refer to our discussion of the ideas in the proof of Theorem B.17. In particular, in the context of the
proof of Theorem B.17,M 1 lied on one side of its boundary. Hence, in a proof-by-contradiction-
argument, it was shown to be possible to construct a spacelike hypersurface portion Σ ′ containing
some point in∂M 1, which allows one to extend the solution to the larger regionM 1 ∪D(Σ ′),
whereD(Σ ′) is the domain of dependence of Σ ′ inV(Vis the connected component ofM 1 ∩M 2
containing Σ 0). In the context of Fig. 9, this extension procedure fails in the sense thatM 1 is
already defined on both sides of∂M 1,so there is no “room” left to extend.
58To clarify, the reason we call the gradient blow-up of the non-unique solutions of Theorem B.23 more “severe”
than a shock – even though gradient blow-up also occurs in shock formation for compressible Euler – is because the
components of the metric itself (456) blow up. In contrast, our main result (10.1) implies that the acoustical metric
g(448) extends as a well-defined continuous Lorentzian metric to the entire singular boundary and crease. More
generally, “shock formation” results for solutions to a quasilinear wave equation of the form (h −1)αβ[∂Φ]∂α∂βΦ =
N[Φ, ∂ϕ] such as [7,8] feature blow-up of∂ 2Φ, and not∂Φ.

Figure 9.The non-unique GHDs of [19] for the same data on Σ 0. The GHDM 1 is
the subset ofR 1+1 between the dashed red curves and Σ 0, but doesnotinclude the
solid red curve or black dot. That is,M 1 is the union of the red and purple regions
without the solid red curve and black dot. The GHDM 2 is the subset ofR 1+1
between the dashed blue curves and Σ 0, but doesnotinclude solid blue curve or
black dot. That is,M 2 is the union of the blue and purple regions without the solid
blue curve and black dot.M 1 ∩M 2 is disconnected. The connected component of
M1 ∩M 2 that contains Σ0 is denoted byV. Φ 1 and Φ2 agree onV. Both solutions
are singular at the black dot, but∂Φ 1 blows up precisely on the portion of the solid
red curve inM 1 ∩M 2 while Φ2 is smooth there. In contrast,∂Φ 2 blows up on the
portion of the solid blue curve inM 1 ∩M 2 while Φ 1 is smooth there, and both
solutions have the same data: Φ 1|Σ0 = Φ2|Σ0 and∂ tΦ1|Σ0 =∂ tΦ2|Σ0.
The last sentence of Theorem B.23 is a direct example of a breakdown of classical determinism
due to non-uniqueness. By this, we mean that neither the equation nor the dynamics indicate a
preferred choice between Φ 1 and Φ 2. In addition, we note that the non-unique GHDs of Theo-
rem B.23 are not maximal. However, by Theorem B.13, there exists an MGHD (M Max
1 ,Φ Max
1 ) that
is an extension of (M 1,Φ 1). Similarly, there exists an MGHD (M Max
2 ,Φ Max
2 ) that extends (M2,Φ 2)
and therefore cannot be the same as (M Max
1 ,Φ Max
1 ). In particular, equation (455) admits smooth
data that lead to non-unique MGHDs.
We close this section by highlighting the following interesting open problem:
Given smooth data for equation (447), can one prove the existence of an MGHD
withoutinvoking Zorn’s lemma, just as Sbierski did for Einstein’s equations [37].
B.4.5.There might not even be a single unique GHD.The discussion in Sect. B.4.4 was primarily
about non-uniqueness of a maximal GHD for a wave equation. In principle, even worse pathologies
could arise. As an example, we highlight the following conjecture, which was originally stated as
[19, Conjecture 4.81]:
Conjecture B.24.There exist quasilinear wave systems of the form(447)and smooth initial data
for which there doesnotexist any unique GHD in the sense of Def. B.10; see Fig. 10.
129

[PDF page 130]
Σ Σ Σ
t
n
Figure 10.This figure, which is adapted from [19, Figure 16], depicts a potential
mechanism for resolving Conj. B.24. The blue regions represent a sequence of GHDs
{(Mn,Φ n)}∞
n=1 of the same initial data posed on a Cauchy hypersurface Σ. Asn→
+∞, the causal regions of the GHDs get arbitrarily close to the Cauchy hypersurface.
Hence, there would not exist anyopen set containing Σ on which the data determines
a unique solution.
Informally speaking, for an example that verifies Conj. B.24, it would not be possible to uniquely
classically solve the Cauchy problem in any neighborhood – however small – of the Cauchy hyper-
surface on which the data are posed.
